.navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Catholic_Church_in_England_and_Wales_sidebar" class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Catholic_Church_in_England_and_Wales_sidebar&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Pope Adrian IV</b> (<a href="/wiki/Latin_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin language">Latin</a>: <i lang="la">Adrianus IV</i>; born <b>Nicholas Breakspear</b> (or <b>Brekespear</b>);<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEden2006292_1-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEden2006292-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1100</span><sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;note 1&#93;</a></sup>&#160;&#8211;&#32;1 September 1159, also <b>Hadrian IV</b>),<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEParry199718_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEParry199718-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> was head of the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a> and ruler of the <a href="/wiki/Papal_States" title="Papal States">Papal States</a> from 4 December 1154 to his death in 1159. He is the only Englishman to have been pope.
</p><p>Adrian was born in <a href="/wiki/Hertfordshire" title="Hertfordshire">Hertfordshire</a>, <a href="/wiki/England" title="England">England</a>, but little is known of his early life. Although he does not appear to have received a great degree of schooling, while still a youth he travelled to France where he was schooled in <a href="/wiki/Arles" title="Arles">Arles</a>, studying law. He then travelled to <a href="/wiki/Avignon" title="Avignon">Avignon</a>, in the south, where he joined <a href="/w/index.php?title=Abbey_of_Saint-Ruf,_Avignon&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Abbey of Saint-Ruf, Avignon (page does not exist)">St Ruf</a><span class="noprint" style="font-size:85%; font-style: normal;">&#160;&#91;<a href="https://fr.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbaye_Saint-Ruf_d%27Avignon" class="extiw" title="fr:Abbaye Saint-Ruf d&#39;Avignon">fr</a>&#93;</span>. There he became a <a href="/wiki/Canons_regular" class="mw-redirect" title="Canons regular">canon regular</a> and was eventually appointed abbot. He travelled to <a href="/wiki/Rome" title="Rome">Rome</a> several times, where he appears to have caught the attention of <a href="/wiki/Pope_Eugene_III" title="Pope Eugene III">Pope Eugene III</a>, and was sent on a mission to <a href="/wiki/Catalonia" title="Catalonia">Catalonia</a> where the <a href="/wiki/Reconquista" title="Reconquista">Reconquista</a> was attempting to reclaim land from the Muslim <a href="/wiki/Al-Andalus" title="Al-Andalus">Al-Andalus</a>. Around this time his abbey complained to Eugene that Breakspear was too heavy a <a href="/wiki/Disciplinarian" class="mw-redirect" title="Disciplinarian">disciplinarian</a>, and in order to make use of him as a <a href="/wiki/Papal_legate" title="Papal legate">papal legate</a> as well as to pacify his monks, he was appointed <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Albano" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Albano">Bishop of Albano</a> some time around 1149.
</p><p>As bishop, Breakspear was soon sent on another diplomatic mission, this time to <a href="/wiki/Scandinavia" title="Scandinavia">Scandinavia</a>. In the middle of <a href="/wiki/Civil_war_era_in_Norway" title="Civil war era in Norway">a civil war</a>, Breakspear reorganised the Church in Norway and then moved on to Sweden. Here, he was very much acclaimed by the people, and when he left, <a href="/wiki/Chronicle" title="Chronicle">chroniclers</a> called him a saint. Breakspear returned to Rome in 1154; Eugene's successor <a href="/wiki/Pope_Anastasius_IV" title="Pope Anastasius IV">Pope Anastasius IV</a> had died only a few weeks previously. For reasons now unknown, but possibly at his predecessor's request, Breakspear was elected next pope by the cardinals. He was unable to complete his coronation service, however, because of the parlous state of politics in Rome, which at the time was a den of '<a href="/wiki/Heresy" title="Heresy">heresy</a>' and <a href="/wiki/Republicanism" title="Republicanism">republicanism</a>. Adrian decisively restored the papal authority there, but his other major policy issue—relations with the newly crowned <a href="/wiki/Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Holy Roman Emperor">Holy Roman Emperor</a>, <a href="/wiki/Frederick_I,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" class="mw-redirect" title="Frederick I, Holy Roman Emperor">Frederick I</a>—started off badly and got progressively worse. Each party, as a result of a particular aggravating incident, found something to condemn the other for. As a result, Adrian entered into an alliance with the <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_emperor" class="mw-redirect" title="Byzantine emperor">Byzantine emperor</a>, <a href="/wiki/Manuel_I_Komnenos" title="Manuel I Komnenos">Manuel I Komnenos</a> who was keen to re-assert his authority in the south of Italy, but was unable to do so due to the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Sicily" title="Kingdom of Sicily">Norman kings' occupation of the region</a>, now under <a href="/wiki/William_I_of_Sicily" title="William I of Sicily">William I of Sicily</a>.
</p><p>Adrian's alliance with the Byzantine emperor came to nothing, as William decisively defeated Manuel and forced Adrian to come to terms at the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Benevento" title="Treaty of Benevento">Treaty of Benevento</a>. This alienated Emperor Frederick even more, as he saw it as a repudiation of <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Constance_(1153)" title="Treaty of Constance (1153)">their existing treaty</a>. Relations soured further when Frederick laid claim to a large swathe of territory in northern Italy. Adrian's relations with his country of birth, however, seem to have remained generally good. Certainly, he showered <a href="/wiki/St_Albans_Abbey" class="mw-redirect" title="St Albans Abbey">St Albans Abbey</a> with privileges, and he appears to have forwarded <a href="/wiki/Henry_II_of_England" title="Henry II of England">King Henry II's</a> policies where he could. Most famously, in 1158 Adrian is supposed to have granted Henry the <a href="/wiki/Papal_bull" title="Papal bull">papal bull</a> <i><a href="/wiki/Laudabiliter" title="Laudabiliter">Laudabiliter</a></i>, which is thought to have authorised Henry to invade Ireland. Henry did not do so, however, for another 14 years, and scholars are uncertain whether the bull ever existed.
</p><p>Following Adrian's death at <a href="/wiki/Anagni" title="Anagni">Anagni</a>, there was uncertainty as to who to succeed him, with both pro- and anti-imperial cardinals voting for different candidates. Although <a href="/wiki/Pope_Alexander_III" title="Pope Alexander III">Pope Alexander III</a> officially took over, the subsequent election of an <a href="/wiki/Antipope" title="Antipope">antipope</a> led to a 22-year-long schism. Scholars have debated Adrian's pontificate widely. Much of a positive nature—his building programme and reorganisation of papal finances, for example—has been identified, particularly in the context of such a short reign. He was also up against powerful forces out of his control, which, while he never overcame them, he managed effectively.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Adrian_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The son of Richard Breakspear,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> his family was a relatively humble one.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESummerson2004_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESummerson2004-6">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> The exact year of his birth is unknown but he was probably around 55 years old on his election.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENorwich2011_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorwich2011-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Little is known<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;note 2&#93;</a></sup> of his background, and that which is, comments Brooke, "savour[s] of gossip rather than sober history.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrooke20034_8-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrooke20034-8">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> He was probably born in or around the Hertfordshire town of <a href="/wiki/St_Albans" title="St Albans">St Albans</a>. As a result, much of that that is thought to be known may well be mythological "tradition woven at the great abbey" there.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> It has been suggested that he was born in the hamlet of <a href="/wiki/Bedmond" title="Bedmond">Bedmond</a> in the parish of <a href="/wiki/Abbots_Langley" title="Abbots Langley">Abbots Langley</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> Much of what is known is brought to historians by the writings of <a href="/wiki/Boso_of_Santa_Pudenziana" class="mw-redirect" title="Boso of Santa Pudenziana">Cardinal Boso</a> and <a href="/wiki/William_of_Newburgh" title="William of Newburgh">William of Newburgh</a>, both of whom were, however, writing over 30 years after Breakspear's death.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPoole1969291_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPoole1969291-12">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> As a result, notes Poole, there is a dearth of information—and especially dates—for Breakspear's life until his election as pope, and "all that can be said is that the dates commonly given are in every instance wrong".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPoole1969293_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPoole1969293-13">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> The English <a href="/wiki/Chronicle" title="Chronicle">chronicler</a> <a href="/wiki/Matthew_Paris" title="Matthew Paris">Matthew Paris</a> says he came from <a href="/wiki/Abbots_Langley" title="Abbots Langley">Abbots Langley</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMaxwell-Stuart199797_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaxwell-Stuart199797-14">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> although Paris mistakenly ascribes to his father the name <a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_de_Camera&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Robert de Camera (page does not exist)">Robert de Camera</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrooke20035_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrooke20035-15">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> Robert may have been a clerk<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMaxwell-Stuart199797_14-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaxwell-Stuart199797-14">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;note 3&#93;</a></sup> although Sayers suggests that Paris' claim that Robert was a priest is probably correct, as is the likelihood that he later became a monk.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;note 4&#93;</a></sup> As such, there are grounds for believing Nicholas to have been illegitimate.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrooke20034_8-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrooke20034-8">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> Nicholas had a brother called either Ranulf or Randall, a <a href="/wiki/Clerk" title="Clerk">clerk</a> in <a href="/wiki/Feering" title="Feering">Feering</a>, Essex.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> Paris is also the source for Nicholas' surname being Breakspear.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Paris recounts a story that Nicholas was rejected by <a href="/wiki/Abbot_of_St_Albans" title="Abbot of St Albans">Abbot Robert de Gorron</a> from taking his <a href="/wiki/Novitiate" title="Novitiate">novitiate</a> at the abbey, although as Poole points out, the story is demonstrably incorrect as Robert did not become abbot until 1151.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPoole1969292_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPoole1969292-21">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> Sayers, suggests that, true or not, during and after Breakspear's pontificate, "certainly St Albans fed upon the story of the local boy who had made good".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> William of Newburgh reports that Nicholas was too poor to receive anything more than a rudimentary education, and Brooke speculates that he travelled to France to learn the skills of a clerk. This was, he notes, a normal path to preferment in the 12th century, although it was more unusual for those that did so to have Breakspear's inauspicious background.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrooke200319_19-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrooke200319-19">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> He may have become a <a href="/wiki/Canon_(priest)" class="mw-redirect" title="Canon (priest)">canon</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Augustinian_priory" class="mw-redirect" title="Augustinian priory">Augustinian priory</a> in <a href="/wiki/Merton,_Surrey" class="mw-redirect" title="Merton, Surrey">Merton, Surrey</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> Poole subscribes to this theory, citing a letter to Breakspear when pope in which he is reminded that "your worship was wont to speak" of Merton in conversation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPoole1969293_13-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPoole1969293-13">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Move_to_France_and_promotion">Move to France and promotion</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Adrian_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Move to France and promotion">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:St_Alban%27s_cathedral_-_geograph.org.uk_-_1344.jpg" class="image"><img alt="Colour photograph of St Albans Abbey, now a cathedral" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/49/St_Alban%27s_cathedral_-_geograph.org.uk_-_1344.jpg/220px-St_Alban%27s_cathedral_-_geograph.org.uk_-_1344.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/49/St_Alban%27s_cathedral_-_geograph.org.uk_-_1344.jpg/330px-St_Alban%27s_cathedral_-_geograph.org.uk_-_1344.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/49/St_Alban%27s_cathedral_-_geograph.org.uk_-_1344.jpg/440px-St_Alban%27s_cathedral_-_geograph.org.uk_-_1344.jpg 2x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="480" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:St_Alban%27s_cathedral_-_geograph.org.uk_-_1344.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>St Albans Abbey, now a Cathedral, pictured in 2005</div></div></div>
<p>The next point at which Breakspear can be positively identified is in the French town of <a href="/wiki/Arles" title="Arles">Arles</a>, where he continued his studies<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEParry199718_4-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEParry199718-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> in <a href="/wiki/Canon_law" title="Canon law">canon law</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2004189_n.80_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2004189_n.80-22">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> and probably under the masters of Roman law also.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEgger200320_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEgger200320-23">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> On completion of his studies, he travelled south and became a <a href="/wiki/Canon_regular" title="Canon regular">canon regular</a> at the priory of <a href="/w/index.php?title=Abbey_of_Saint-Ruf,_Avignon&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Abbey of Saint-Ruf, Avignon (page does not exist)">St Ruf</a><span class="noprint" style="font-size:85%; font-style: normal;">&#160;&#91;<a href="https://fr.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbaye_Saint-Ruf_d%27Avignon" class="extiw" title="fr:Abbaye Saint-Ruf d&#39;Avignon">fr</a>&#93;</span>,<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;note 5&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Avignon" title="Avignon">Avignon</a>. He was soon appointed <a href="/wiki/Prior" title="Prior">prior</a><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPoole1969291_12-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPoole1969291-12">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> and then abbot.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> While still a canon, in 1140 he appears to have written a charter in <a href="/wiki/Barcelona" title="Barcelona">Barcelona</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPoole1969294_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPoole1969294-27">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> However, there appear to have been complaints that he was overly strict,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENorwich2011_7-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorwich2011-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> and the monks rebelled.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPoole1969294_27-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPoole1969294-27">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> As a result, he was summoned to Rome;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEParry199718_4-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEParry199718-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENorwich2011_7-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorwich2011-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> a temporary peace was established, but it was not long until the monks rebelled again.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPoole1969294_27-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPoole1969294-27">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> Breakspear may have visited Rome three times while at St Ruf—"each time with more conspicuous success"—and which would have consumed many months of his time.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPoole1969294–295_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPoole1969294–295-28">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Sayers suggests that it was while Breakspear was at St Ruf that he attracted the attention of <a href="/wiki/Pope_Eugenius_III" class="mw-redirect" title="Pope Eugenius III">Pope Eugenius III</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> who saw in him useful leadership qualities.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEParry199718_4-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEParry199718-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;note 6&#93;</a></sup> It is known that in 1147, while Eugenius was in <a href="/wiki/Vico_Pancellorum" title="Vico Pancellorum">Vico</a> he granted one "N. abbot of St Rufus".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPoole1969294_27-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPoole1969294-27">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> It was probably in 1148 that Breakspear met who would become his good friend, John of Salisbury, in <a href="/wiki/Rheims" class="mw-redirect" title="Rheims">Rheims</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrooke20037_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrooke20037-31">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> and soon after when <sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMorris1989190_25-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorris1989190-25">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBolton2003a75_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBolton2003a75-32">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> Eugenius appointed him <a href="/wiki/Cardinal-Bishop" class="mw-redirect" title="Cardinal-Bishop">Cardinal-Bishop</a> of <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Suburbicarian_Diocese_of_Albano" title="Roman Catholic Suburbicarian Diocese of Albano">Albano</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> making Adrian at the time only the second Englishman to have been promoted to that rank.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2004355_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2004355-33">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;note 7&#93;</a></sup> in which capacity he attended the <a href="/wiki/Council_of_Reims_(1148)" title="Council of Reims (1148)">Council of Reims</a> in November 1148.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2004180_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2004180-35">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith200333_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith200333-36">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> Poole suggests that Breakspear's promotion was Eugenius' method of alleviating the monks' complaints, as Eugenius told them to "go forth [and] elect you a father with whom ye can or will live in peace; he [Breakspear] shall no longer be a burthen to you".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPoole1969294_27-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPoole1969294-27">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> When Breakspear was later pope, however, he seemed to favour St Ruf well, for example authorising them to send a delegation to the <a href="/wiki/Cathedral_chapter" title="Cathedral chapter">chapter</a> of <a href="/wiki/Pisa_Cathedral" title="Pisa Cathedral">Pisa Cathedral</a> to cut stone and columns. The chapter was requested, says Egger, to "help them in every possible way to conduct their business".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEgger200327_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEgger200327-37">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Poole questions the reasoning for Breakspear's episcopal promotion. Not only was his abbey an obscure one, with little political value or great endowment, but Breakspear's reasons for attending the Papal court were unlikely to have enabled him to make a name for himself. Indeed, on at least one occasion it was in response to a summons concerning his behaviour. However, suggest Poole, a possible explanation may have its roots in Breakspear's residency at Merton.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPoole1969295_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPoole1969295-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;note 8&#93;</a></sup> Duggan notes that the Cardinal Bishopric of Albino was part of the Pope's inner circle, which she suggests makes his rapid elevation to such a sensitive position all the more remarkable and indicative of the now-unrecognisable qualities that Eugenius saw in him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b201_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b201-39">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Voyage_to_Catalonia">Voyage to Catalonia</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Adrian_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Voyage to Catalonia">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>It was probably at the Council of Reims that Eugenius selected Breakspear for a mission to Catalonia,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith200333_36-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith200333-36">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;note 9&#93;</a></sup> possibly as a kind of unofficial legate to the crusaders.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEConstable195325_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEConstable195325-42">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> Breakspear met <a href="/wiki/Ramon_Berenguer_IV,_Count_of_Barcelona" title="Ramon Berenguer IV, Count of Barcelona">Ramon Berenguer IV, Count of Barcelona</a> who had been waging the latest campaign against the Moors since 1147. Although no records exist to show Breakspear was involved in the campaign itself, he was heavily involved in the reorganisation and administration of the <a href="/wiki/Cistercian_Order" class="mw-redirect" title="Cistercian Order">Cistercian Order</a>, as well as arbitrating disputes within its congregation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith200335_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith200335-43">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> It is likely that he was present at the <a href="/wiki/Lleida" title="Lleida">Siege of Lleida</a> during the summer of 1149. He is less likely to have still been there in October, when it fell, as he had returned to Rome by December. However, he may well have brought news of another successful siege—that of <a href="/wiki/Tortosa" title="Tortosa">Tortosa</a>—which would have been particularly welcome to the "bruised and battered" papacy of Eugenius, says Damian Smith.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith200336_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith200336-44">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> Smith also notes that Breakspear's lengthy absence from St Ruf may have been a source of complaint by his monks, "but this was surely not of prime importance to the Pope".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith200336_44-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith200336-44">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> Egger suggests, however, that Breakspear's Catalonian mission was of great benefit to St Ruf, which became the blueprint for the religious houses created by Berenguer in the wake of the retreating Muslim empire.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEgger200325_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEgger200325-45">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;note 10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Around mid-1152, he was despatched to <a href="/wiki/Scandinavia" title="Scandinavia">Scandinavia</a> as <a href="/wiki/Papal_legate" title="Papal legate">Papal legate</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Visit_to_Scandinavia.2C_1152"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Visit_to_Scandinavia,_1152">Visit to Scandinavia, 1152</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Adrian_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Visit to Scandinavia, 1152">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Trondheim-cathedral-1.jpg" class="image"><img alt="modern photo of Trondheim Cathedral" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Trondheim-cathedral-1.jpg/170px-Trondheim-cathedral-1.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="127" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Trondheim-cathedral-1.jpg/255px-Trondheim-cathedral-1.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Trondheim-cathedral-1.jpg/340px-Trondheim-cathedral-1.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2580" data-file-height="1932" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Trondheim-cathedral-1.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Trondheim Cathedral, as seen in 2005</div></div></div>
<p>Former <a href="/wiki/Canon_residentiary" class="mw-redirect" title="Canon residentiary">Canon residentiary</a> of St Albans Abbey, Andes Bergquist has described Breakspear's journey to northern Europe as "one of the better documented" of his career. It is possible that Boso—from whom much of the information comes—was in his entourage, although this is not certain.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBergquist200341,_42_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBergquist200341,_42-47">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> On his arrival, Norway was in a <a href="/wiki/Civil_war_era_in_Norway" title="Civil war era in Norway">state of civil war</a> and the authority of the King, <a href="/wiki/Inge_I_of_Norway" title="Inge I of Norway">Inge I</a>, was neither strong nor respected. Breakspear reconciled the warring factions—albeit temporarily—and restored the monarchy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2004349_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2004349-48">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> although no official record of his instructions survives, Bergquist suggests that they can be inferred from his actions: to divide the existing Archbishopric of Lund—which covered both Norway and Sweden–into two distinct national metropolitans, to arrange payment of Peter's Pence and to generally reorganise the church along Italian and European lines.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBergquist200342_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBergquist200342-49">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Breakspear may have travelled to Norway via France and England<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBergquist200343_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBergquist200343-50">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>—where, Sayers speculates, he could have recruited merchant contacts who knew the region.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAndåsEkrollHaugPetersen20079_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAndåsEkrollHaugPetersen20079-51">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> His mission may have been kept quiet, as Bergquist notes his arrival seems to have been unexpected: Archbishop Eskil of Lund had recently left to visit France, and the King of Norway was on a military campaign.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBergquist200344_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBergquist200344-52">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> His first stop was Norway. At some point, Breakspear presided over a council at <a href="/wiki/Nidaros" title="Nidaros">Nidaros</a>. This council, says Robinson, "strengthened the economic position of the church and the social status of the clergy".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2004349_48-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2004349-48">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> Its timing though is difficult to ascertain, says Bergquist: Autumn 1152 seems to allow too little time to organise such a major council following his arrival, yet much later and the depth of a Norwegian winter is even more unlikely.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBergquist200344–45_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBergquist200344–45-53">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The focal point of the <a href="/wiki/Olaf_II_of_Norway#Liturgical_cult" title="Olaf II of Norway">cult of St Olaf</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2004349_48-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2004349-48">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> Nidaros had until that point been only an episcopate.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAndåsEkrollHaugPetersen20079_51-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAndåsEkrollHaugPetersen20079-51">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> Adrian's council was intended to promulgate <a href="/wiki/Canon_law_of_the_Catholic_Church" title="Canon law of the Catholic Church">canons</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> To this end Breakspear made Nidaros a geographically extensive ecclesiastical <a href="/wiki/Province" title="Province">province</a>, covering the whole of Norway, Iceland and Greenland, as well as the <a href="/wiki/Faroe_Islands" title="Faroe Islands">Faroe</a>, <a href="/wiki/Orkney" title="Orkney">Orkney</a> and <a href="/wiki/Shetland_Island" class="mw-redirect" title="Shetland Island">Shetland Islands</a>. Breakspear also authorised the expansion of what was to become Europe's <a href="/wiki/Trondheim_Cathedral" class="mw-redirect" title="Trondheim Cathedral">most northerly medieval cathedral</a>, and its largest.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAndåsEkrollHaugPetersen20079_51-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAndåsEkrollHaugPetersen20079-51">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> While in Norway he founded three <a href="/wiki/Cathedral_schools" class="mw-redirect" title="Cathedral schools">cathedral schools</a>, at Nidaros in 1152 and two more at <a href="/wiki/Bergen" title="Bergen">Bergen</a> and <a href="/wiki/Hamar" title="Hamar">Hamar</a> the following year.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESjåvik200863_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESjåvik200863-54">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> His work in Norway earned him the praise of contemporary Icelandic writer and politician, <a href="/wiki/Snorri_Sturluson" title="Snorri Sturluson">Snorri Sturluson</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b200_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b200-55">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>If the Council of Nidaros was held in the early months of 1153, suggests Bergquist, then it appears that Breakspear sailed to Sweden as soon as it was concluded.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBergquist200344_52-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBergquist200344-52">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> His activities in Sweden followed a similar course to those in Norway.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBergquist200346_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBergquist200346-56">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> He called another council, this time at <a href="/wiki/Link%C3%B6ping" title="Linköping">Linköping</a>, which reorganised the Swedish church under the <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_Lund" class="mw-redirect" title="Archbishop of Lund">Archbishop of Lund</a> (it had previously been subject to German patriarchy).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> He also received permission from the <a href="/wiki/Swedish_monarchy" class="mw-redirect" title="Swedish monarchy">Swedish monarchy</a> to introduce <a href="/wiki/Peter%27s_pence" class="mw-redirect" title="Peter&#39;s pence">Peter's pence</a><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> and to reduce the influence of the lay community on the church generally.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBergquist200346_56-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBergquist200346-56">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> His visit to Sweden was recorded by contemporary chroniclers and published in the 13th century.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPhelpstead2001xxxiv_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPhelpstead2001xxxiv-57">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> Similarly to what he had done in Norway with Trondheim,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2004349_48-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2004349-48">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> Adrian attempted to create an archepiscopal see for Sweden. This was opposed by one of the three provinces, <a href="/wiki/Gothland" class="mw-redirect" title="Gothland">Gothland</a>, and the venture came to nothing.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEden2006292–293_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEden2006292–293-58">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> According to Bergquist, Breakspear ""was taken aback by this unseemly conflict, and declared that neither people deserved this highest ecclesiastical honour".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBergquist200346_56-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBergquist200346-56">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> Indeed, he suggests that it is possible that Breakspear's plans fell through thanks to the machinations of the recently returned Archbishop Eskil. Eskil, having discovered that he had lost half his archepiscopate in his absence, may have stirred up the Swedes' and Goths' rivalries to ensure against losing any more. In the event, Breakspear appears to have repaired relations with Eskil, assuring him that Eskil would receive far more than he had lost. As a result, he placed Eskil in charge of the new Swedish metropolitan.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBergquist200346_56-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBergquist200346-56">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Duggan describes Adrian's legation in the north has a "diplomatic triumph",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a153_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a153-59">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> being so successful, says Sayers, "that he was later seen as the apostle of Scandinavia".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> Boso later lauded how Breakspear brought "peace to the kingdoms, law to the barbarians, tranquillity to the monasteries, order to the churches, discipline to the clergy and a people pleasing to God, devoted to good works".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2004349_48-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2004349-48">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> He successfully introduced a new Scandinavian <a href="/wiki/Tythe" class="mw-redirect" title="Tythe">tythe</a>—the <i>denarium sancti Petri</i>, or payment to St Peter—a financial acknowledgement by the Scandinavian church of Papal primacy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESheehy196148–49_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESheehy196148–49-60">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> Breakspear, argues the scholar Andrew Beck, "gave the Swedish church its hierarchy and its attachment to Rome".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBeck1940383_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBeck1940383-61">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;note 11&#93;</a></sup> He left Scandinavia in autumn 1154; he seems to have left a generally good impression in the region: A later <a href="/wiki/Kings%27_sagas" title="Kings&#39; sagas">saga</a> refers to Breakspear as "the good cardinal...now considered a saint".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBergquist200341_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBergquist200341-64">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> On his return to Rome he found Pope Eugenius had died the previous year, and that his successor had followed him only a few weeks before; the <a href="/wiki/College_of_Cardinals" title="College of Cardinals">College of Cardinals</a> was seeking a successor.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBergquist200341_64-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBergquist200341-64">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Political_background">Political background</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Adrian_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Political background">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Plan_of_Rome_in_the_Middle_Ages_-_Historical_Atlas_by_William_R._Shepherd,_1923.jpg" class="image"><img alt="map of medieval rome" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/Plan_of_Rome_in_the_Middle_Ages_-_Historical_Atlas_by_William_R._Shepherd%2C_1923.jpg/220px-Plan_of_Rome_in_the_Middle_Ages_-_Historical_Atlas_by_William_R._Shepherd%2C_1923.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="230" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/Plan_of_Rome_in_the_Middle_Ages_-_Historical_Atlas_by_William_R._Shepherd%2C_1923.jpg/330px-Plan_of_Rome_in_the_Middle_Ages_-_Historical_Atlas_by_William_R._Shepherd%2C_1923.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/Plan_of_Rome_in_the_Middle_Ages_-_Historical_Atlas_by_William_R._Shepherd%2C_1923.jpg/440px-Plan_of_Rome_in_the_Middle_Ages_-_Historical_Atlas_by_William_R._Shepherd%2C_1923.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1016" data-file-height="1063" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Plan_of_Rome_in_the_Middle_Ages_-_Historical_Atlas_by_William_R._Shepherd,_1923.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Medieval Rome; the <a href="/wiki/Leonine_City" title="Leonine City">Leonine City</a> is to the northwest of the city, outside the Leonine Wall (in blue)</div></div></div>
<p>Discussing the broader political context of the time, the historian <a href="/w/index.php?title=Anne_Duggan&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Anne Duggan (page does not exist)">Anne Duggan</a> argues that "the Pope was not master of his own house".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a109_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a109-65">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> Likewise, <a href="/wiki/Walter_Ullmann" title="Walter Ullmann">Walter Ullmann</a> has argued that the age was a radical one, in which the <a href="/wiki/Temporal_power_of_the_Holy_See" title="Temporal power of the Holy See">temporal power</a>—specifically, the "educated lay element"—was encroaching upon traditional spiritual realms.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955233_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955233-66">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>The age in which Adrian took office was one that witnessed profound changes in all spheres of life, and change always brings in its train restlessness, crises, stress and tension, caused by the attempted displacement of the old by the new. New forces were released which had hitherto had no opportunity of asserting themselves and which challenged the traditional scheme of things vigorously.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955233_66-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955233-66">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Eugenius had died in July 1153.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarber199299_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarber199299-67">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> His successor, <a href="/wiki/Anastasius_IV" class="mw-redirect" title="Anastasius IV">Anastasius IV</a>, had been already elderly when elected to succeed him,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMaxwell-Stuart199797_14-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaxwell-Stuart199797-14">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> and only ruled for a year.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> Comparing the two, the <a href="/wiki/Popular_historian" class="mw-redirect" title="Popular historian">popular historian</a> <a href="/wiki/John_Julius_Norwich" title="John Julius Norwich">John Julius Norwich</a> comments that the former "was old and ineffectual, concerned chiefly with his own self-glorification"; Adrian, though, was "a man of very different calibre".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENorwich1970172_29-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorwich1970172-29">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> Anastasius died on 3 December 1154,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> and by which time, Breakspear had returned to Rome.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> Even before the death of Eugenius, argues Barber, "a new and formidable figure had appeared" on the political scene. The <a href="/wiki/Hohenstaufen" title="Hohenstaufen">Hohenstaufen</a> <a href="/wiki/Frederick_Barbarossa" title="Frederick Barbarossa">Frederick Barbarossa</a> had been elected <a href="/wiki/Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Holy Roman Emperor">Holy Roman Emperor</a> on 4 March 1152.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarber1992101_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarber1992101-68">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> Barbarossa and Eugenius had contracted, at the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Constance" class="mw-redirect" title="Treaty of Constance">Treaty of Constance</a>, to unite against both William of Sicily and the Roman Commune.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarber1992101_68-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarber1992101-68">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> <sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;note 12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Ullmann has identified four major areas of concern for Adrian at the beginning of his pontificate: the city of Rome under Arnold of Brescia, the new emperor who was marching towards Rome for his coronation, his counterpart in the east whose army had recently invaded southern Italy, and restlessness among the Pope's own vassals in his patrimony.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955235_72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955235-72">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> By the time of Adrian's consecration, the city of Rome was a major player in Papal-Aristocratic regional politics.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWickham2015446_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWickham2015446-73">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> Under the governance of a republican commune since 1144,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETwyman200349_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETwyman200349-74">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> Pope Eugenius had recognised it the following year. While the city was usually happy to acknowledge the feudal lordship of the Pope, it was—even compared to other Italian <a href="/wiki/City_states" class="mw-redirect" title="City states">city states</a>—both "unusually self-aware, and also unusually idiosyncratic" compared to others.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWickham2015446_73-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWickham2015446-73">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;note 13&#93;</a></sup>  The commune was hostile to the Papacy. 
The Papacy was weak in the city of <a href="/wiki/Rome" title="Rome">Rome</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Heretic" class="mw-redirect" title="Heretic">heretic</a>, <a href="/wiki/Arnold_of_Brescia" title="Arnold of Brescia">Arnold of Brescia</a>, had ruled since 1146<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaumgartner2003b31_76-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaumgartner2003b31-76">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> and was popular. He also had the support of the <a href="/wiki/Commune_of_Rome" title="Commune of Rome">Roman Commune</a>. The popularity of Arnold directly translated into hostility towards the popes.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Chichele_Professor" class="mw-redirect" title="Chichele Professor">Chichele Professor</a> <a href="/wiki/Chris_Wickham" title="Chris Wickham">Chris Wickham</a> describes the relationship between the Pope and the lords of his Patrimony as one in which, because "their lords did not by any means all look to Rome [they] had to be coaxed back or brought back by force".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWickham201539_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWickham201539-77">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> Papal politics was beset by problems at home and abroad.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson199678_69-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson199678-69">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> The election of Adrian IV as Pope, comments the papal scholar <a href="/w/index.php?title=Ian_S._Robinson&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Ian S. Robinson (page does not exist)">Ian S. Robinson</a>—and, indeed, the elections of his immediate predecessors—"took place in the shadow of the communal revolution in Rome".<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;note 14&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>From Eugenius, Adrian inherited what Walter Ullmann has called a "mutual assistance pact" with the Emperor, the Treaty of Constance, signed the year of Eugenius' death. For the popes, its most important aspect was the stipulation that the crowning of the next emperor was contingent on expelling Arnold of Brescia from Rome.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann200350_82-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann200350-82">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> It also assured each party of the other's support against both King William in Sicily and the Byzantine Empire when necessary.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETabacco2004430_83-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETabacco2004430-83">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> The treaty was confirmed by Adrian in January 1155.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMagdalino199358_84-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMagdalino199358-84">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> Eugenius was a believer in the <a href="/wiki/Pope_Gregory_VII" title="Pope Gregory VII">Gregorian doctrine</a> of <a href="/wiki/Papal_supremacy" title="Papal supremacy">Papal supremacy</a>, stating that Christ "gave to St Peter the keys of the kingdom of heaven, the power of both the earthly and the heavenly empire".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2004322_85-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2004322-85">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> From the beginning of his reign, Barbarossa sought to present himself as the heir to a long, established line of Roman Emperors, and likewise that his empire was a continuation of <a href="/wiki/Roman_Empire" title="Roman Empire">theirs</a>. The historian Anne A. Latowsky explains how this was the cause of tension in the European polity:<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELatowsky2013140_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELatowsky2013140-86">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Despite grandiose allusions to the German inheritance of the universal dominion of Augustus, the Roman Empire continued to be, as it had been for centuries, a primarily theoretical concept based on an idealized notion of the protection of all Christendom...such claims often clashed with papal pretensions to the primary role as guardians of a unified and universal Christendom<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELatowsky2013140_86-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELatowsky2013140-86">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Norwich argues that, by now, whatever the public statements of either Papal or Imperial party, they were mutually antagonistic, and had been for many years. Even before Adrian's pontificate, he says, no peace treaty was strong enough to unite them for long: "The days when it had been realistic to speak of the two swords of Christendom were gone—gone since Gregory VII and Henry IV had hurled depositions and anathemas at each other nearly a hundred years before".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENorwich1970181_87-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorwich1970181-87">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> The situation, suggest Duggan, was "a minefield", for the Pope, and Adrian had to negotiate it.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a109_65-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a109-65">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>It was the ambition of the Emperor of the Eastern Empire, Manuel I Kommenus, to reunite both Empires under one crown, and, as such, he wished to be crowned by the Pope in Rome, as Western emperors were.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVasiliev1973476_88-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVasiliev1973476-88">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;note 15&#93;</a></sup> The death of Roger II presented Manuel with an opportunity he could not afford to let by, argues Professor <a href="/wiki/Paul_Magdalino" title="Paul Magdalino">Paul Magdalino</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMagdalino199358_84-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMagdalino199358-84">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> The Kingdom of Sicily had been recognised by <a href="/wiki/Innocent_II" class="mw-redirect" title="Innocent II">Innocent II</a> in 1143, notes the Italianist <a href="/wiki/Graham_Loud" title="Graham Loud">Graham Loud</a>, but "relations with the Papacy remained fraught".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELoud201237_91-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELoud201237-91">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> The previous King of Sicily, <a href="/wiki/Roger_II_of_Sicily" title="Roger II of Sicily">Roger II</a>, had ruled his kingdom with an iron fist, and his nobility chafed,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWieruszowski196929_92-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWieruszowski196929-92">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> particularly the large number he had effectively dispossessed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBirk2016211_93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBirk2016211-93">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> His son was less interested than his father in the minutiae of government, and when Roger died in 1154 they took advantage of the new king and rebelled. This was of interest to the Papacy as the rebels were willing to ally themselves with anyone for their purpose.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWieruszowski196929_92-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWieruszowski196929-92">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Election.2C_1154"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Election,_1154">Election, 1154</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Adrian_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Election, 1154">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:292px;"><video id="mwe_player_0" poster="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5d/Adrian_IV%27s_itinerary%2C_1154-1159.ogv/290px--Adrian_IV%27s_itinerary%2C_1154-1159.ogv.jpg" controls="" preload="none" class="thumbimage" width="290" height="163" data-durationhint="80" data-mwtitle="Adrian_IV&#39;s_itinerary,_1154-1159.ogv" data-mwprovider="wikimediacommons"><source src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/5/5d/Adrian_IV%27s_itinerary%2C_1154-1159.ogv" type="video/ogg; codecs=&quot;theora, vorbis&quot;" data-title="Original Ogg file, 1,920 × 1,080 (2.15 Mbps)" data-shorttitle="Ogg source" data-width="1920" data-height="1080" data-bandwidth="2154501" data-framerate="25" /></video>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Adrian_IV%27s_itinerary,_1154-1159.ogv" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Simplified itinerary of Adrian IV, 1154–1159.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBolton2003b179_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBolton2003b179-94">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> Neither distances nor location to scale. Dates indicate Adrian's presence not of arrival departure.</div></div></div>
<p>It was Breakspear's being "in the right place at the right time", suggests the Papal librarian <a href="/wiki/Bradford_Lee_Eden" title="Bradford Lee Eden">Bradford Lee Eden</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEden2006293_95-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEden2006293-95">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> that led to his election as pope on Saturday, 4 December 1154,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955237_96-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955237-96">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> although Duggan argues that he must also have had exceptional qualities, both to reach the rank he had and as seen in his Scandinavian trip<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a152_97-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a152-97">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup>—or as William of Newburgh later wrote, "raised as if from the dust to sit in the midst of princes.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBoltonDuggan2003273_98-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoltonDuggan2003273-98">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> Events moved rapidly:<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955237_96-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955237-96">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> the period was one of great crisis for the papacy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a155_99-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a155-99">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> Adrian was enthroned on the 5th and crowned in <a href="/wiki/Old_St._Peter%27s_Basilica" title="Old St. Peter&#39;s Basilica">St Peter's</a> on the 6th.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955237_96-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955237-96">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;note 16&#93;</a></sup> His election, said Boso, "happened—not without divine council—that they unanimously agreed" on Adrian.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson199678_69-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson199678-69">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> To date, Adrian has been the only English pope.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEden2006293_95-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEden2006293-95">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;note 17&#93;</a></sup> He was one of the few popes of his era who did not need <a href="/wiki/Consecration" title="Consecration">consecrating</a> on his election, as he was already a bishop.<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;note 18&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to Boso, Breakspear had to be forced "against his will" into the Papal throne.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETwyman200349_74-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETwyman200349-74">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> He took the name Adrian IV, possibly in honour of <a href="/wiki/Adrian_I" class="mw-redirect" title="Adrian I">Adrian I</a>, who revered <a href="/wiki/St_Alban" class="mw-redirect" title="St Alban">St Alban</a><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2004355_n.243_104-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2004355_n.243-104">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> and first granted the abbey of that name its privileges.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBolton2003a75_32-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBolton2003a75-32">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> It was, suggests Julius Norwich, " wise choice, for energy and force were desperately needed".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENorwich1970177_105-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorwich1970177-105">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> Although he had been elected unanimously from among the cardinals, the role of the Roman people was ignored. Thus relations between the Pope and his city were poor from the beginning,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaumgartner2003b31_76-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaumgartner2003b31-76">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> as were relations between Adrian and the King of Sicily, who controlled much of southern Italy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEden2006293_95-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEden2006293-95">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> Relations with the commune were so bad that Adrian was forced to remain in the <a href="/wiki/Leonine_city" class="mw-redirect" title="Leonine city">Leonine city</a><sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;note 19&#93;</a></sup> and was thus unable to immediately complete the enthronement ceremony, as tradition dictated, by making his <i><a href="/wiki/Adventus_(ceremony)" title="Adventus (ceremony)">adventus</a></i> into Rome itself. In the event, Adrian was required to remain there for the next four months.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETwyman200349_74-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETwyman200349-74">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> As a result, although he had been consecrated, he had not been crowned in the ceremony <i>dies coronae</i> at the Lateran which gave him not his title but gave him feudal title of the papal lands. It is probable that, due to problematic relations with the Romans, he did not receive his crown until the following Easter.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETwyman200367_107-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETwyman200367-107">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Neutralises_Rome">Neutralises Rome</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Adrian_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Neutralises Rome">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Due to Arnold's presence in Rome, there were a number of acts of religious significance that it was impossible to perform, such as the ceremony of the <i><a href="/wiki/Sede_stercoraria" class="mw-redirect" title="Sede stercoraria">sede stercoraria</a></i>, the physical claiming of the <a href="/wiki/Curule_seat" title="Curule seat">curule seats</a> of Saints Peter and Paul.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955238_108-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955238-108">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> Soon after Adrian's election, a cardinal was badly beaten up by Roman republicans.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Adrian was no more popular with the people or Commune of Rome than his immediate predecessors, so at Easter the next year he departed for <a href="/wiki/Viterbo" title="Viterbo">Viterbo</a>. His "primary task", argues Sayers, "was to control the Emperor" Frederick Barbarossa. Barbarossa had only recently been elected to the Imperial throne and for their own reasons, Pope and Emperor needed each other. Adrian needed Barbarossa's military support<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> against William, (known as "The Bad")<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEParry199718_4-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEParry199718-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/William_I_of_Sicily" title="William I of Sicily">King of Sicily</a>, who was threatening the <a href="/wiki/Papal_patrimony" class="mw-redirect" title="Papal patrimony">Papal patrimony</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;note 20&#93;</a></sup> For his part, the Emperor needed Adrian to perform the traditional <a href="/wiki/Coronation_of_the_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Coronation of the Holy Roman Emperor">imperial coronation service</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Adrian took a hardline against the Roman commune.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaumgartner2003b31_76-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaumgartner2003b31-76">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> He threatened to place the city under <a href="/wiki/Interdict_(Catholic_canon_law)" class="mw-redirect" title="Interdict (Catholic canon law)">interdict</a> for protecting Arnold, whom the hierarchy condemned as a heretic.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarber1992101_68-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarber1992101-68">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> This strategy successfully drove a wedge between the commune and Arnold, who was expelled.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a153_59-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a153-59">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> He followed through with this threat following the beating of one of his cardinals<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Norwich has called this "an act of breath-taking courage", considering that Adrian was a foreign Pope of only a few weeks' tenure, who "knew the city and its increasingly xenophobic inhabitants hardly at all and was able to rely on little or no popular support".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENorwich1970172_29-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorwich1970172-29">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;note 21&#93;</a></sup> on the <a href="/wiki/Via_Sacra" title="Via Sacra">Via Sacra</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECollins2009235_112-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECollins2009235-112">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> Rome was forced to submit to the Pope, and Arnold of Brescia was expelled.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Although he had managed to restore Papal authority in the city, he was unable to eradicate the principle of republicanism, and the commune remained as the governing body.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWhalen2014125_113-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWhalen2014125-113">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Neutralises_Arnold">Neutralises Arnold</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Adrian_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Neutralises Arnold">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Adrian angled for the support of the Emperor in capturing the heretic Arnold.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Arnold was captured by Imperial troops<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016144_114-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016144-114">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> in summer 1155. Arrested and tried in a Papal court<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016144_114-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016144-114">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> for rebellion rather than heresy, he was <a href="/wiki/Hanging" title="Hanging">hanged</a> and his body burnt.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaumgartner2003b32_115-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaumgartner2003b32-115">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> Adrian claimed that Arnold's execution had been on the initiative of the <a href="/wiki/Prefect" title="Prefect">prefect</a> of Rome, but some contemporary observers, such as <a href="/wiki/Gerhoh_of_Reichersberg" title="Gerhoh of Reichersberg">Gerhoh of Reichersberg</a>, suspected Adrian of ordering the execution himself.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMorris1989406_116-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorris1989406-116">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> The Emperor's willingness to assist the Pope in his own city, and help him crush his enemies, was an explicit recognition from Barbarossa of the Pope's possession of Rome.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016144_114-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016144-114">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> Papal relations with the lords of Campania were already tense, as they, in the Pope's view, were little more than <a href="/wiki/Robber_baron_(feudalism)" title="Robber baron (feudalism)">robber barons</a>, who both fought among each other and robbed pilgrims from the south on their way to Rome.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEden2006293_95-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEden2006293-95">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Imperial_trouble_at_Sutri.2C_late_1155"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Imperial_trouble_at_Sutri,_late_1155">Imperial trouble at Sutri, late 1155</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Adrian_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Imperial trouble at Sutri, late 1155">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Frederick_I,_Holy_Roman_Emperor.jpg" class="image"><img alt="contemporary colour illustration of Frederick I" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/04/Frederick_I%2C_Holy_Roman_Emperor.jpg/170px-Frederick_I%2C_Holy_Roman_Emperor.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="204" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/04/Frederick_I%2C_Holy_Roman_Emperor.jpg/255px-Frederick_I%2C_Holy_Roman_Emperor.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/0/04/Frederick_I%2C_Holy_Roman_Emperor.jpg 2x" data-file-width="280" data-file-height="336" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Frederick_I,_Holy_Roman_Emperor.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Frederick I, Holy Roman Emperor, as depicted in a 12th-century chronicle</div></div></div>
<p>Barbarossa had received the <a href="/wiki/Iron_Crown_of_Lombardy" class="mw-redirect" title="Iron Crown of Lombardy">Iron Crown of Lombardy</a>—as <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Italy_(Holy_Roman_Empire)" title="Kingdom of Italy (Holy Roman Empire)">King of Italy</a>—in <a href="/wiki/Pavia" title="Pavia">Pavia</a>, but also wished to receive his <a href="/wiki/Imperial_Crown_of_the_Holy_Roman_Empire" title="Imperial Crown of the Holy Roman Empire">Imperial Crown</a> from the Pope.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEden2006293_95-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEden2006293-95">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> Adrian originally saw the Emperor as protector and defender of the church.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarber1992101_68-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarber1992101-68">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> Both parties, notes Ullmann, were unpopular in Rome:<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann2003122_117-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann2003122-117">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Because of fear of Roman hostility and disturbances the imperial coronation on 18 June 1155 had to be performed secretly on a Saturday (instead of on a Sunday as usual) in order to mislead the Romans, all this being somewhat incongruous for "the lord of the world and master of Rome" who was there with his armed forces.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann2003122_117-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann2003122-117">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote> 
<p>To this end, Adrian and Barbarossa met at <a href="/wiki/Sutri" title="Sutri">Sutri</a> in early June 1155.<sup id="cite_ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119">&#91;note 22&#93;</a></sup> This soon, says Sayers, "turned out to be a spectacular contest between the two to gain propagandist supremacy".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> Adrian, reports an Imperial chronicler, was there "with the entire Roman Church, met us joyfully, paternally offered us holy consecration and complained to us of the injuries he had suffered at the hands of the Roman populace".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016140_118-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016140-118">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> Barbarossa later recalled the ceremony in a letter to the Eastern Emperor in 1189:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>For in the city of Rome, which is known as the lady and head of the world, we received the crown and rule over all of Christianity from the altar of St Peter, Prince of the Apostles, and were solemnly anointed with the oil of majesty by the lord Pope Adrian, the successor to St Peter, before our fellows, and our name is held to be famous and glorious because of this".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELoud2010152_120-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELoud2010152-120">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Adrian may have been caught off-balance by the Emperor's swift entry into Italy and the speed with which he approached Rome. The dispute was sparked by Barbarossa's unwillingness to act as the Pope's <a href="/wiki/Strator" title="Strator">strator</a>;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERotondo-McCord2001284_121-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERotondo-McCord2001284-121">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> lead the Pope's horse by the bridle—or to assist Adrian in dismounting—as was traditionally expected. In response, the Pope refused the Emperor the <a href="/wiki/Kiss_of_peace" title="Kiss of peace">kiss of peace</a>;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> the Emperor was still willing to perform the duty of kissing Adrian's feet, though.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson1996464_122-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson1996464-122">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> These were minor affronts at best, says Barber, "but in an age so highly conscious of symbolic acts", took on a greater political import.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarber1992101_68-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarber1992101-68">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The confusion at Sutri may have been accidental,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMorris1989191_123-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorris1989191-123">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> but Frederick also took offence at a mural<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a130_124-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a130-124">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> in the <a href="/wiki/Lateran" title="Lateran">Lateran</a> of his predecessor Luthar which described the Emperor as a <a href="/wiki/Liegeman" class="mw-redirect" title="Liegeman">liegeman</a> of the Pope.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarber1992102_125-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarber1992102-125">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> The painting was inscribed with the verse
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><div class="poem">
<p>The king comes before the gates, first swearing to<br />
&#160;&#160;uphold the rights of the city.<br />
Then he becomes the liegeman of the pope;<br />
&#160;&#160;he accepts the crown, which the pope gives.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJong2013163_126-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJong2013163-126">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
</div></blockquote>
<p>Indignant,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJong2013163_126-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJong2013163-126">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> Barbarossa made a "friendly reproach" to the Pope.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson1996464_122-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson1996464-122">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> In a letter to a German bishop, he explained, "it began with a picture. The picture became an inscription. The inscription seeks to become an authoritative utterance. We shall not endure it, we shall not submit to it."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJong2013163_126-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJong2013163-126">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> Adrian told Barbarossa he would have it removed,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarber1992102_125-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarber1992102-125">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-127" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-127">&#91;note 23&#93;</a></sup> "lest so trifling a matter might afford the greatest men in the world an occasion for dispute and discord.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016206_128-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016206-128">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> In the event, Adrian did not,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarber1992102_125-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarber1992102-125">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> and by 1158 Imperial commentators were describing the matter of the painting and its inscription as the fundamental cause of the dispute between Pope and Emperor.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016207_129-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016207-129">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> Adrian, says Freed, was "perplexed" at the Emperor's refusal to offer him squire service: he "dismounted and seated himself on a folding stool".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016141_130-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016141-130">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> Barbarossa, if he wished to be crowned, had limited options against the Pope. He took advice from councillors<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> based on records of "the more ancient princes and especially those who had come with King Lothar to Pope Innocent".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson1996464_122-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson1996464-122">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> An entire day was spent<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECollins2009229_131-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECollins2009229-131">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> inspecting both "old documents",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016141_130-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016141-130">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-132" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-132">&#91;note 24&#93;</a></sup> and hearing from those of his entourage who had been present at the 1131 ceremony.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2004321_133-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2004321-133">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> The Pope's party saw this as a sign of aggression, and deserted Adrian for the security of a nearby castle.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECollins2009229_131-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECollins2009229-131">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Imperial_coronation.2C_1155"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Imperial_coronation,_1155">Imperial coronation, 1155</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Adrian_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Imperial coronation, 1155">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The Emperor was, though, eventually persuaded, performed the necessary services.
