He was eventually crowned in <a href="/wiki/Nepi" title="Nepi">Nepi</a> on 18 June.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEden2006293_95-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEden2006293-95">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-134" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-134">&#91;note 25&#93;</a></sup> Peace was maintained at Nepi, however, and both Pope and Emperor dined together, wearing their crowns in a joint celebration of the Feast of <a href="/wiki/Saints_Peter_and_Paul" class="mw-redirect" title="Saints Peter and Paul">Saints Peter and Paul</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson1996464_122-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson1996464-122">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> There was much rejoicing, and contemporaries went so far as to proclaim that "a single state had been created from two princely courts".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed201625–26_135-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed201625–26-135">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> Ullmann, on the other hand, argues that, not only was the Emperor's power clearly derivative of the Pope but that Adrian himself had further diluted it in his rendition of the coronation ceremony.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955240–241_136-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955240–241-136">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-138">&#91;note 26&#93;</a></sup> Nor was there an official enthronement for the new emperor.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955242_139-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955242-139">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>This ceremony, says Sayers, was arguably a new version of the traditional one, which now "highlight[ed] the difference between the anointing of a mere layman and that of a priest".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> Previously, Emperors had been anointed on the head, as a priest was; this time, Adrian anointed Barbarossa between the shoulders.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> Further, the Pope invested him with a sword, which emphasised the Emperor's role—as Adrian saw it—as the defender of the Papacy and its privileges.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> Adrian, on the other hand, disallowed his <a href="/wiki/Chancery_(medieval_office)" title="Chancery (medieval office)">chancery</a> from addressing the Emperor by either of his preferred titles, <i>augustus semper</i> or <i>semper augustus</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed201656_140-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed201656-140">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup> It may be that Adrian had been frightened by the Emperor's decisive approach on Rome<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016144_114-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016144-114">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup>—Duggan notes he "impos[ed] obedience on recalcitrant cities and proclaim[ed] the resumption of Imperial rights" as he did.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a109_65-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a109-65">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> If so, that may have led him to over-reacting the face of a perceived slight, however small.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016144_114-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016144-114">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Following the Imperial coronation, both sides appear to have taken extra care to ensure they abided by the Treaty of Constance. Barbarossa, for example, refused to entertain an embassy from the Roman commune.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMorris1989191_123-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorris1989191-123">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> He did not, however, further perform as Adrian hoped, and did not defend the Papacy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> Indeed, he stayed in Rome only enough time to be crowned, and then left immediately: "dubious protection" for the Pope, comments Barber.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarber1992101_68-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarber1992101-68">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> Before he left, however, his army was drawn into a bloody clash with Rome's citizens,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEden2006293_95-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEden2006293-95">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> incensed at what they saw as a display of Imperial authority in their city.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWhalen2014126_141-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWhalen2014126-141">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> Over 1,000 Romans died.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEden2006293_95-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEden2006293-95">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> The Senate continued revolting in Rome and William of Sicily remained entrenched in the Patrimony. Adrian was trapped between King and Emperor.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> Freed suggests that Barbarossa's failure to suppress the Roman commune for Adrian led the Pope to believe the Emperor had broken the Treaty of Constance.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016152_142-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016152-142">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> Further, on the Emperor's march north, his army <a href="/wiki/Looting" title="Looting">sacked</a> and <a href="/wiki/Demolition" title="Demolition">razed</a> the town of <a href="/wiki/Spoleto" title="Spoleto">Spoleto</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEden2006293_95-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEden2006293-95">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> Adrian left Rome also, as his relations with the commune were still too fragile for him to be able to guarantee his safety following the Emperor's departure.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWhalen2014126_141-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWhalen2014126-141">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> As a result, the Pope was left in "virtual exile" in Viterbo,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> and relations between the two declined further.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarber1992101_68-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarber1992101-68">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Normans.2C_Greeks_and_Apulians"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Normans,_Greeks_and_Apulians">Normans, Greeks and Apulians</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Adrian_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Normans, Greeks and Apulians">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:12th-century_Italy.png" class="image"><img alt="map of 12th-century Italy" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d3/12th-century_Italy.png/170px-12th-century_Italy.png" decoding="async" width="170" height="202" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d3/12th-century_Italy.png/255px-12th-century_Italy.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d3/12th-century_Italy.png/340px-12th-century_Italy.png 2x" data-file-width="638" data-file-height="759" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:12th-century_Italy.png" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Map of 12th-century Italy, illustrating the respective boundaries between the Papal States and Patrimony and those claimed by King William and Emperor Frederick</div></div></div>
<p>Probably as a result, he responded positively to overtures from the <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_Emperor" class="mw-redirect" title="Byzantine Emperor">Byzantine Emperor</a> <a href="/wiki/Manuel_I_Komnenos" title="Manuel I Komnenos">Manuel I</a>, and also the native barons of Southern Italy, who saw in Adrian's support a chance for them to overthrow William, whom Adrian had recently <a href="/wiki/Excommunicated" class="mw-redirect" title="Excommunicated">excommunicated</a><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> for invading the Papal patrimony.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarber1992101_68-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarber1992101-68">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> The rebellion had started off promisingly, with rebel victories at <a href="/wiki/Bari" title="Bari">Bari</a>, <a href="/wiki/Trani,_Apulia" class="mw-redirect" title="Trani, Apulia">Trani</a> and <a href="/wiki/Andria" title="Andria">Andria</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENorwich1970175_143-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorwich1970175-143">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup> They had already found themselves a powerful ally in Manuel, the Byzantine Emperor, and welcomed anyone—including Adrian—who was hostile to William.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETreadgold1997642_144-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETreadgold1997642-144">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> Their leader, Count Robert of Loritello, had been charged with treason by William but had managed to escape north. William was temporarily struck down with an unknown illness, as the scholar Joshua C. Birk explains, "this brought the enemies of the kingdom of Sicily out of the woodwork"; among them, Adrian excommunicated William.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBirk2016211_93-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBirk2016211-93">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> By 1154, William had captured important towns in the Patrimony.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEden2006293_95-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEden2006293-95">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> In summer 1155 rebellion broke out in southern Italy by the native nobility against their lord, the King of Sicily. One group of rebels, having gained the support of Emperor Manuel, overran <a href="/wiki/Ancona" title="Ancona">Ancona</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson1996464_122-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson1996464-122">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> By winter 1155, suggests Norwich, few contemporaries "would have held out much hope for the future of the Sicilian monarchy".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENorwich1970175_143-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorwich1970175-143">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup> According to Boso, the rebels asked Adrian to come to them as their feudal lord, to act as their spiritual advisor and bless them in their endeavours.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMatthew199262_145-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMatthew199262-145">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup> Adrian, believing that William's kingdom would collapse imminently,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMatthew199265_146-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMatthew199265-146">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> tried to exploit William's weakness and allied with the rebels in September. As it turned out, this was a miscalculation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson1996464_122-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson1996464-122">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> William had already asked Adrian for a peace conference, which the Pope had ("scornfully") rejected.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMatthew199262_145-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMatthew199262-145">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-149" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-149">&#91;note 27&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Alliance_with_Manuel_I.2C_1156"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Alliance_with_Manuel_I,_1156">Alliance with Manuel I, 1156</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Adrian_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Alliance with Manuel I, 1156">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Emperor Manuel I had launched his own military operation against William in southern Italy in 1154.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955235_72-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955235-72">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-151" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-151">&#91;note 28&#93;</a></sup> He found Adrian a willing ally. The Russian historian <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Vasiliev_(historian)" title="Alexander Vasiliev (historian)">Alexander Vasiliev</a> notes that Adrian "expressed his desire 'to help in bringing all the brethren into one church' and compared the eastern church with lost <i>drachma</i>, wandering sheep, and the dead <a href="/wiki/Lazarus_of_Bethany" title="Lazarus of Bethany">Lazarus</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVasiliev1973476_88-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVasiliev1973476-88">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> Adrian's isolation led directly to his concordat with the Eastern Empire in 1156,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarber1992101_68-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarber1992101-68">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> although Duggan emphasises that he was reacting to external political pressures rather than deliberately initiating a new policy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a120_150-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a120-150">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> As a result, says Barber, he "became involved in a fruitless Byzantine plan to overcome the Normans which ended, as so often before when the popes had ventured south in arms, in Norman victory".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarber1992101_68-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarber1992101-68">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> Adrian—as if, says Partner, "the unhappy experiences of at least three popes ha taught the papacy nothing"<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPartner1972190_152-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPartner1972190-152">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup>—organised a papal army comprising Roman and Campagnan nobility and crossed the border into Apulia in September 1155.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPartner1972190_152-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPartner1972190-152">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Although it has been suggested that Manuel offered to pay Adrian a large sum of money in return for ceding him certain Apulian cities, it seems unlikely that this was ever actioned; certainly, notes Duggan, Adrian was wholly against the creation of a Byzantine kingdom on his own doorstep.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a122_153-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a122-153">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> This was in spite of Manuel deliberately not pressing his ancestor's historical claim to south Italy as a whole,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMagdalino2008638_154-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMagdalino2008638-154">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup> and was interested primarily in the coastal areas.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMagdalino199357_155-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMagdalino199357-155">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-157" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-157">&#91;note 29&#93;</a></sup> Initially, his campaign succeeded, and by 1155 he had occupied the area from Ancona to <a href="/wiki/Taranto" title="Taranto">Taranto</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955235_72-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955235-72">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> Byzantine funding enabled Adrian to temporarily restore his vassal <a href="/wiki/Robert_III_of_Loritello" class="mw-redirect" title="Robert III of Loritello">Robert, Count of Loritello</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarber1992236_158-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarber1992236-158">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENorwich1970175_143-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorwich1970175-143">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup> although on one occasion William was able to capture 5,000 pounds (2,300 kilograms) of gold from Manuel that had been destined for the Pope's war chest.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEden2006293_95-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEden2006293-95">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> There was some discussion of an alliance between Roman Pope and Eastern Emperor, and Adrian despatched <a href="/wiki/Anselm_of_Havelberg" title="Anselm of Havelberg">Anselm of Havelberg</a> east to arrange it, although in the event negotiations came to nothing.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECameron201683_159-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECameron201683-159">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup> Magdalino argues that Adrian would not have been interested in an alliance "without the lure of Byzantine gold".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMagdalino1993105_160-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMagdalino1993105-160">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> Although the Byzantine Emperor had sent his army to support the Pope in Italy—and indeed, had subdued the troublesome region of the <a href="/wiki/Balkans" title="Balkans">Balkans</a>—Adrian, argues Sayers, "could not accept any power for the emperor that was not dependent on the pope".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> Ullmann argues that although Adrian was receptive to Manuel's ambition of uniting the Eastern and Western Roman Empires, he did not appreciate the manner in which the offer was made.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955248_161-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955248-161">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> He was particularly averse to Manuel's suggestion that the pope's sword was merely a spiritual force,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> and, suggests Ullmann, "received Manuel's overtures with that scepticism which they deserved".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955248_161-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955248-161">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> Adrian, though, while agreeing on the basic tenet of a single emperor and a single church, believed that it was not a case of the Western church joining that of the East, but the latter returning to the former with the "obedience due to a mother", as he put it in a letter to Manuel. In other words, all Christians, East or West, should be subjugated to the church of St Peter.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955249_162-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955249-162">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Norman_victory">Norman victory</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Adrian_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Norman victory">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Strategically, King William's position was not looking good, and he offered Adrian large sums in financial compensation for the Pope to withdraw his forces. However, the majority of Adrian's curia were averse to holding negotiations with the Sicilians, and the King's offer was rejected somewhat haughtily. This turned out to be a bad mistake.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPartner1972190–191_163-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPartner1972190–191-163">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup> William soon won decisive victories over both Greek and Apulian armies in mid-1156,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> culminating in the final defeat of the Eastern Empire at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Brindisi_(1156)" title="Battle of Brindisi (1156)">Battle of Brindisi</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChrysostomides200918_164-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChrysostomides200918-164">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup> When William soundly defeated the rebels,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson1996464_122-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson1996464-122">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> Adrian—who was by now, even more, bogged down in the problem of Rome<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMatthew199263_165-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMatthew199263-165">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup> and without allies<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENorwich1970176_166-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorwich1970176-166">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup>—had to sue for peace on the King's terms.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson1996464_122-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson1996464-122">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> This was yet another external event—indeed, probably the single most important event of the pontificate she argues—that Adrian had had no way of influencing but had to deal with its consequences, notes Duggan.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a153_59-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a153-59">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> He was effectively captured and forced to come to terms at <a href="/wiki/Benevento" title="Benevento">Benevento</a><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAbulafia198856_167-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbulafia198856-167">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-168" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-168">&#91;note 30&#93;</a></sup> three weeks later.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELoud2004456_169-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELoud2004456-169">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup> This one event, says Duggan, changed Adrian's policy for good, whether or not he liked it.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a110_170-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a110-170">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup> As a result, at the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Benevento" title="Treaty of Benevento">Concordat of Benevento</a>, Adrian had to invest William with the lands he claimed in southern Italy, symbolised by the presentation of the Pope's own <a href="/wiki/Pennon" title="Pennon">pennoned</a> lances and the kiss of peace.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENorwich1970176_166-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorwich1970176-166">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup> The Pope was accepted as William's feudal overlord, while being forbidden from entering Sicily without an invitation from the King,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarber1992236_158-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarber1992236-158">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-171" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-171">&#91;note 31&#93;</a></sup> thereby granting William effectively Legatine authority over the church in his own land.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELoud2004456_169-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELoud2004456-169">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup> For his part, William gave the Pope his <a href="/wiki/Homage_(feudal)" title="Homage (feudal)">homage</a> and contracted to pay an annual tribute<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> and provide military support on request.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWhalen2014126_141-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWhalen2014126-141">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> The treaty conferred extended powers on the Kings of Sicily that they would enjoy for at least the next 40 years, and included powers over ecclesiastical appointments traditionally held by the Popes as the region's feudal lord.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMatthew1992193_172-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMatthew1992193-172">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-173" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-173">&#91;note 32&#93;</a></sup> Adrian's treaty with William angered the Emperor, who took it as a personal slight that Adrian had treated with the two Imperial rivals to Italy and confirmed his view of Adrian's Papal arrogance.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENorwich1970180_174-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorwich1970180-174">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> This, suggests Robinson, sowed the seeds of the disputed election following Adrian's death.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson199679_175-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson199679-175">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2004378_176-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2004378-176">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The defeat of Manuel's army left the Pope vulnerable, and in June 1156 Adrian was forced to come to terms with the Sicilian King.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarber1992101_68-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarber1992101-68">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> This was, however, suggests Robinson, on generous terms, including "homage and fealty, reparation for the recent encroachments on the papal patrimony, help against the Romans, freedom from royal control for the Sicilian church".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2004378_176-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2004378-176">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup> Adrian's new alliance with William exacerbated relations with Barbarossa,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> who believed that Adrian had broken the Treaty of Constance twice over, by allying with both King William and the Byzantine Emperor.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson1996464_122-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson1996464-122">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> Relations between Pope and Emperor were, argues Latowsky, "irreparably damaged.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELatowsky2013168_177-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELatowsky2013168-177">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup> Adrian probably acted as mediator the following year in concluding a peace treaty between William and Manuel.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson1996464_122-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson1996464-122">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> The Emperor attempted to prevent the treaty by sending his most experienced diplomat, Abbot <a href="/wiki/Wibald" title="Wibald">Wibald</a> to intervene, as he probably saw a Sicilian–Byzantine alliance as being directed against him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016195_178-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016195-178">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The alliance with William had probably been strengthened by the Pope's belief that Barbarossa had already broken the Treaty of Constance.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016152_142-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016152-142">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> At the Treaty of Benevento, Adrian was represented by the Cardinals Ubald, Julius and Roland;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMatthew199265_146-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMatthew199265-146">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> the Papacy was forced to cede much valuable land, rights and income to William.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2004367_179-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2004367-179">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup> The Emperor felt personally betrayed: according to the contemporary chronicler <a href="/wiki/Geoffrey_of_Viterbo" class="mw-redirect" title="Geoffrey of Viterbo">Geoffrey of Viterbo</a>, the Pope, "wish[ed] to be an enemy of Caesar".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a107_180-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a107-180">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup> Duggan, however, suggests that the Imperial alliance with the papacy had only ever been a <a href="/wiki/Flag_of_convenience" title="Flag of convenience">flag of convenience</a>, "ready to be discarded when it had served its purpose".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a154_181-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a154-181">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-182" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-182">&#91;note 33&#93;</a></sup> Bolton, meanwhile, suggests that, as Benevento was an Imperial town, the fact that following the treaty he stayed there for eight more months indicates that Adrian was asserting his power.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBolton2003b165_183-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBolton2003b165-183">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Problems_in_translation.2C_1157"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Problems_in_translation,_1157">Problems in translation, 1157</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Adrian_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Problems in translation, 1157">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>By 1157, suggests Whalen, having secured the border with the south (by his alliance with Sicily) and the commune as peaceful as it had been for some time, Adrian was able to reside in Rome again and "stood in a more secure position than any of his predecessors had for decades".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWhalen2014126_141-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWhalen2014126-141">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> They were made worse in 1157 when, in a letter to the Emperor, Adrian referred to the Empire by the Latin term <i>beneficium</i>, which some of Barbarossa's councillors translated as <a href="/wiki/Fief" title="Fief">fief</a>, rather than <a href="/wiki/Benefice" title="Benefice">benefice</a>. This, they claimed, implied that the Pope saw the Empire as subordinate to the Papacy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-185" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-185">&#91;note 34&#93;</a></sup> The Emperor had to personally hold back <a href="/wiki/Otto_I,_Duke_of_Bavaria" title="Otto I, Duke of Bavaria">Otto of Wittelsbach</a> from assaulting the Pope's messengers.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016201_186-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016201-186">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup> Ullmann, however, argues that Adrian's use of the word was "harmless enough...that he conferred the Imperial crown as a favour".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955244_187-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955244-187">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup> Duggan too describes the incident as "at best a diplomatic incident—a <i>faux pas</i>—which suggests carelessness on the part of the drafter".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a129_188-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a129-188">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-189" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-189">&#91;note 35&#93;</a></sup> Historians have disagreed as to the degree of deliberation behind the use of the word. <a href="/wiki/Peter_Munz" title="Peter Munz">Peter Munz</a>, for example, believes it to have been a deliberate provocation, engineered by an anti-Imperial faction within the curia, designed to justify Adrian's treaty with King William. Anne Duggan, on the other hand, suggests this view is "scarcely credible": not only was Adrian in no position of strength from which to threaten Frederick, but he was also aware that the Emperor was planning a campaign against Milan for the following year, and would hardly wish to provoke him into marching on towards the Papal States.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a129_188-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a129-188">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In October 1157, Barbarossa was celebrating his wedding in <a href="/wiki/Besan%C3%A7on" title="Besançon">Besançon</a><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann2003123_190-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann2003123-190">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup> with <a href="/wiki/Diet_of_Besan%C3%A7on" title="Diet of Besançon">an Imperial Diet</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWhalen2014126_141-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWhalen2014126-141">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-191" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-191">&#91;note 36&#93;</a></sup> when he was visited by Papal legates <a href="/wiki/Pope_Alexander_III" title="Pope Alexander III">Roland</a><sup id="cite_ref-193" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-193">&#91;note 37&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/w/index.php?title=Bernard,_Cardinal-Bishop_of_Porto&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Bernard, Cardinal-Bishop of Porto (page does not exist)">Bernard</a>. Theirs was an important mission<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson199680_194-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson199680-194">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup> bringing personal letters from Adrian,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarber1992102_125-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarber1992102-125">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> and they were met "with honour and kindness, claiming (as they did) to be the bearers of good tidings".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016202_195-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016202-195">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup> The Pope complained about the lack of activity in discovering who attacked <a href="/wiki/Eskil_of_Lund" title="Eskil of Lund">Eskil</a>, Archbishop of Lund while he travelled through Imperial territory.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarber1992102_125-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarber1992102-125">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> Eskil, complained Adrian, had been captured somewhere "in the German lands...by certain godless and infamous men", and Frederick had made no attempt to secure his release.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016203_196-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016203-196">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-199" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-199">&#91;note 38&#93;</a></sup> Adrian's letter, suggests Godman, both upbraids the Emperor for "dissimulation" and "negligence" while accusing <a href="/wiki/Rainald_of_Dassel" title="Rainald of Dassel">Rainald of Dassel</a> of being a "wicked counsellor ",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGodman2000197_200-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGodman2000197-200">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup> although Duggan describes it more as a "mild rebuke".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a127_198-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a127-198">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup> Barber comments that "the tone is that of one who is surprised and a little hurt that, having treated Frederick so affectionately and honourably, he had not had a better response, but the actual words used to express these sentiments gave rise to immediate offence".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarber1992102_125-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarber1992102-125">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> Adrian's defence of Eskil of Lund contributed further to the decline in his relationship with Barbarossa.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2004350_201-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2004350-201">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup> Adrian's choice of occasion on which to rebuke the Emperor was bound to offend him, argues Norwich.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENorwich1970180_174-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorwich1970180-174">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> But even if unintentional, argues Freed, the Pope should have instructed his delegates to meet with Barbarossa privately rather than in the open. Equally provocative, Freed suggests, was Adrian's later assertion that letters which criticised the Emperor's behaviour were somehow to his advantage.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016202–203_202-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016202–203-202">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup> Adrian's "sharp" words also contributed to the Emperor's advisors increasing discontent with his messengers. The Pope had also ordered that, before any negotiations took place, the Emperor's council would accept Adrian's letters "without any hesitation...as though proceeding from our mouth".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016205_203-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016205-203">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup> The cardinals appear to have worsened their reception by calling Frederick "brother".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a132_204-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a132-204">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Emperor was also exasperated to find, on ordering the legates' quarters searched, blank parchments with the Papal seal attached. This he understood to mean that the legates had intended to present supposedly direct instructions from the Pope when they felt it necessary.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016208_205-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016208-205">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup> Barbarossa claimed that he held his crowns directly from God and that Adrian "did not understand his Petrine commission if he thought otherwise".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAbulafia198863_206-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbulafia198863-206">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup> Following promulgation of Adrian' letter, says Godman, "there was uproar".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGodman2000197_200-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGodman2000197-200">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup> Worse, says Barbarossa's contemporary <a href="/wiki/Chronicler" class="mw-redirect" title="Chronicler">chronicler</a> <a href="/wiki/Otto_of_Freising" title="Otto of Freising">Otto of Freising</a>, the legates compounded the insult by asking those present "from whom the does he have the empire, if not from our lord the pope?" The two <a href="/wiki/Ecclesiast" class="mw-redirect" title="Ecclesiast">ecclesiasts</a> were then nearly beaten up, but the Emperor enabled their swift escape.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarber1992102_125-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarber1992102-125">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Retranslation">Retranslation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Adrian_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Retranslation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In June 1158, representatives of both sides met in the Imperial town of <a href="/wiki/Augsburg" title="Augsburg">Augsburg</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016250_207-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016250-207">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup> Adrian attempted to pacify the Emperor and claimed that he meant, not "fief", but "good deed": "Among us <i>beneficium</i> means not a fief but a good deed", he wrote.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2004323_208-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2004323-208">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup> Barber suggests, though, that "his explanation was far from convincing".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarber1992102_125-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarber1992102-125">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> On the other hand, notes the Emperor's biographer <a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Freed&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="John Freed (page does not exist)">John Freed</a>, Barbarossa was <a href="/wiki/Illiterate" class="mw-redirect" title="Illiterate">illiterate</a>, and required everything translated. He was thus in constant danger of relying on mistranslations, and it is possible that this happened at Besançon.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed201633_209-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed201633-209">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-211" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-211">&#91;note 39&#93;</a></sup> Taken at face value, this phrase appeared to assert that Adrian was the Emperor's feudal overlord.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECollins2009239_212-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECollins2009239-212">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup>
Latowsky argues that the mistranslation was a deliberate ploy by Barbarossa's <a href="/wiki/Archchancellor" title="Archchancellor">Archchancellor</a> Rainald of Dassel—whom she describes as a "multilingual provocateur"—whose Chancery was waging a propaganda war against Adrian.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELatowsky2013161–162_213-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELatowsky2013161–162-213">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-215" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-215">&#91;note 40&#93;</a></sup> The Pope had earlier condemned Reinauld's election as Archbishop of Cologne believed Rainauld to be nothing less than the Devil's agent.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGodman2000221_n.114,_198_216-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGodman2000221_n.114,_198-216">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-218" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-218">&#91;note 41&#93;</a></sup>
Latowsky suggests that Rainauld had intended to cause trouble between Emperor and Pope. If this was the case, he succeeded, as Barbarossa was only just restrained from sending an army against Adrian.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELatowsky2013162_219-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELatowsky2013162-219">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup> The Emperor did make a public declaration against Adrian, though, calling for his deposition on the grounds that, as the son of a priest, he was an uncanonical pope. Ullmann notes that canonicity "was indubitably a double-edged weapon; if Adrian was an uncanonical pope, then Frederick was an uncanonical emperor, and that seems the only reason why this point was not pressed further".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955245_220-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955245-220">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup> Duggan summarises Adrian's Augsburg letter as being concomitant to one's interpretation of the original offence, noting that "the context...determines everything". While Munz views the Augsburg outcome as a "humiliating" retreat by Adrian, Duggan argues that, if one does not view the Besançon letter as deliberately provocative, "then there was no withdrawal from that provocation".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a130_124-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a130-124">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Adrian's choice of words may also have been a "calculated ambiguity", suggests Abulafia,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAbulafia198863_206-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbulafia198863-206">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-222" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-222">&#91;note 42&#93;</a></sup>  and in the event, Adrian never publicly acknowledged which of the interpretations he had actually intended. This would have allowed him to suggest the Emperor has misunderstood him while allowing the Pope to intimate to his own church that the Emperor was indeed a Papal vassal.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016204_210-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016204-210">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup> Adrian "trivialised" Barbarossa's anger with irony, commenting that "this should not have vexed the heart of even one in lowly station, to say nothing of so great a man".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016213_223-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016213-223">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup> The Augsburg meeting seems to have improved relations between Pope and Emperor. As Freed notes, though, "the fundamental question...remained unresolved", and any improvement in relations was temporary, as they fell out again later that year over the appointment of the next <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_Ravenna" class="mw-redirect" title="Archbishop of Ravenna">Archbishop of Ravenna</a>. This revived the question of their respective roles, as the nominations were split between each sides' preferences;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016250_207-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016250-207">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup> in the event, the Imperial candidate—Guido of Biandrate—was elected against Adrian's wishes.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a134_224-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a134-224">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup> There was also increasing disagreement over the traditional <i><a href="/wiki/Jura_regalia" title="Jura regalia">fodrum</a></i> Imperial taxation levied in north Italy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWhalen2014127_225-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWhalen2014127-225">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Imperial_claims_to_north_Italy">Imperial claims to north Italy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Adrian_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Imperial claims to north Italy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Adrian's opposition to Guido of Biandrate's appointment had so incensed the Emperor that he no longer placed the Pope's name before his own in their correspondence, as had been a traditional sign of honour.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a134_224-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a134-224">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup> Furthermore, he began aggressively asserting his claims over <a href="/wiki/Lombardy" title="Lombardy">Lombardy</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWhalen2014127_225-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWhalen2014127-225">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup> and in 1159 the <a href="/wiki/Diet_of_Roncaglia" title="Diet of Roncaglia">Diet of Roncaglia</a><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcBrien1997_226-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBrien1997-226">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup> issued a series of decrees claiming extensive lands in north Italy.<sup id="cite_ref-228" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-228">&#91;note 43&#93;</a></sup> This caused sufficient concern that the cities of <a href="/wiki/Milan" title="Milan">Milan</a><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarber1992103_229-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarber1992103-229">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup>—which Barbarossa had already "half-destroyed", says Ullmann<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann2003124_192-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann2003124-192">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup>—<a href="/wiki/Brescia" title="Brescia">Brescia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Piacenza" title="Piacenza">Piacenza</a><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarber1992103_229-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarber1992103-229">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Crema,_Lombardy" title="Crema, Lombardy">Crema</a> (which had also suffered a "brutal siege", notes Duggan)<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a137_230-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a137-230">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup> approached Adrian for aid.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarber1992103_229-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarber1992103-229">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup> Since the lands concerned were part of the Papal fiefdom,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcBrien1997_226-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBrien1997-226">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup> Adrian, in <a href="/wiki/Bologna" title="Bologna">Bologna</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a134_224-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a134-224">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup> rejected Barbarossa's claim and gave him 40 days in which to withdraw them, on pain of excommunication.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarber1992103_229-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarber1992103-229">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup> However, Adrian's intervention in a quarrel between the Emperor and the Lombard towns may, suggests the classicist <a href="/wiki/Peter_Partner" title="Peter Partner">Peter Partner</a>, "may have been inevitable, but it was to be one of the most explosive issues of its age".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPartner1972200_231-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPartner1972200-231">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Duggan has emphasised the severity of the situation facing Adrian: accepting Frederick's claims, she says, would have entailed Adrian effectively "abandoning the whole Italian church".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a137_230-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a137-230">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup> Adrian also had counter-demands. Frederick was to desist from sending envoys to Rome without papal permission, that he should only be paid the Imperial tax from his Italian lands while in Italy and that those papal lands in north Italy be returned to the church. Adrian, says Duggan, "received short thrift".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a136_232-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a136-232">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup> In the event Adrian died before his 40-day term expired.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarber1992103_229-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarber1992103-229">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup> As relations between Emperor and Pope worsened, Barbarossa took to placing his own name before that of Adrian in their correspondence, as well as addressing the Pope <a href="/wiki/Royal_we" title="Royal we">in the singular</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016110_233-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016110-233">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup> By now, suggests Duggan, Adrian was viewed with contempt by the Emperor.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a138_234-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a138-234">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Relations_with_England">Relations with England</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Adrian_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Relations with England">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Pope Adrian, comments Sayers, "was not unmindful of the interests and well-being of his English homeland",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> and Robinson identifies his pontificate as "the period in which
English influence was strongest in the papal curia".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2004355_33-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2004355-33">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> Adrian remained faithful to the cult of St Alban and often promoted King Henry's political ambitions when he could.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2004355_33-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2004355-33">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> For example, suggests Brooke, following his lengthy stay with Adrian, John of Salisbury seems to have acquired the belief that he would at some point receive a cardinalate. However, John fell out with King Henry for a now-unknown reason, and Adrian—probably wishing to promote his friend but essentially a diplomat and a realist—could not afford to alienate his only major supporter in northern Europe.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrooke200312_235-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrooke200312-235">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup> Adrian also favourably received at least two curial embassies from St Albans in 1156 and 1157.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBolton2003a75_32-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBolton2003a75-32">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-237" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-237">&#91;note 44&#93;</a></sup> In 1156 Adrian ordered King Henry II to appoint an otherwise unknown Hugh to a London <a href="/wiki/Prebend" class="mw-redirect" title="Prebend">prebend</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2007156_n.3_238-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2007156_n.3-238">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup> He wrote to <a href="/wiki/Roger_de_Pont_L%27%C3%89v%C3%AAque" title="Roger de Pont L&#39;Évêque">Roger</a>, <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_York" title="Archbishop of York">Archbishop of York</a> two months after Adrian's election confirming the Papal Legates in their offices.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPoole1902704–705_239-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPoole1902704–705-239">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Adrian had been absent from England since 1120, and it should not be assumed that he bore an automatic affection for the country which, in <a href="/wiki/Richard_Southern" class="mw-redirect" title="Richard Southern">Richard Southern</a>'s words, had given him "no reasons to cherish warm feelings" about it.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERichter1974205_240-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERichter1974205-240">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup> However, in 1156, when John of Salisbury—"in circumstances which otherwise remain obscure"—had fallen into disgrace with the English King, Adrian regularly petitioned Henry for his friend's reinstatement. This was eventually won, but had taken a year to achieve.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2007158–159_241-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2007158–159-241">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup> Anne Duggan, of <a href="/wiki/King%27s_College,_London" class="mw-redirect" title="King&#39;s College, London">King's College, London</a>, describes Anglo-Papal relations at this time as "not so much to a policy, perhaps, but to persistent intervention...and to a degree of acceptance, willing or not, on the part of Church authorities".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2007163_242-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2007163-242">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup> However, Adrian was willing to intervene in English church affairs when it suited, as in February 1156 when he threatened <a href="/wiki/Nigel_(bishop_of_Ely)" title="Nigel (bishop of Ely)">Nigel</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Ely" title="Bishop of Ely">Bishop of Ely</a> with suspension from office<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPoole1969271_243-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPoole1969271-243">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup> over what the <a href="/wiki/Art_historian" class="mw-redirect" title="Art historian">art historian</a> <a href="/wiki/Charles_Reginald_Dodwell" title="Charles Reginald Dodwell">C. R. Dodwell</a> has called Nigel's having "stripped-down, sold, or used as security, a quite astounding number of Ely's monastic treasures".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDodwell1982220–221_244-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDodwell1982220–221-244">&#91;200&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Among other patronages, he confirmed the nuns of <a href="/wiki/Neasham_Priory" title="Neasham Priory">St Mary's Priory</a>, <a href="/wiki/Neasham" title="Neasham">Neasham</a> in possession of their church.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJ._A._H.192674_245-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJ._A._H.192674-245">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup> and granted St Albans Abbey "a large dossier of privileges and directives" exempting it from the jurisdiction of its episcopal master, <a href="/wiki/Robert_de_Chesney" title="Robert de Chesney">Robert de Chesney</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Lincoln" title="Bishop of Lincoln">Bishop of Lincoln</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2007166_246-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2007166-246">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup> He also confirmed the primacy of the Archbishop of York over Scottish bishops and his independence from the Archbishop of Canterbury.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> He also granted papal protection—"free and immune from all subjection except to the Roman pontiff"—to Scottish towns, such as that of <a href="/wiki/Kelso,_Scottish_Borders" title="Kelso, Scottish Borders">Kelso</a> in 1155.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBroun2007138–139_247-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBroun2007138–139-247">&#91;203&#93;</a></sup> He also, on occasion, sent his young protégés to the court of King Henry to learn the aristocratic arts of <a href="/wiki/Hunting" title="Hunting">hunting</a>, <a href="/wiki/Falconry" title="Falconry">falconry</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Martial_arts" title="Martial arts">martial arts</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStrickland201635_248-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStrickland201635-248">&#91;204&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Adrian, suggests the papal scholar <a href="/w/index.php?title=Brenda_M._Bolton&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Brenda M. Bolton (page does not exist)">Brenda M. Bolton</a>, had a particularly "special relationship" with his "home abbey" of St Albans, demonstrated in his generous and wide-ranging <a href="/wiki/Privilege_(canon_law)" class="mw-redirect" title="Privilege (canon law)">privilege</a> <i>Incomprehensibilis</i>, published in Benevento on 5 February 1156.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBoltonDuggan2003312–313_249-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoltonDuggan2003312–313-249">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-252" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-252">&#91;note 45&#93;</a></sup> With this grant, Adrian allowed the abbot the right to wear <a href="/wiki/Pontificals" class="mw-redirect" title="Pontificals">pontificals</a>, thereby effectively removing the abbot from the jurisdiction of Robert de Chesney, his bishop. The monks were also allowed to elect the abbot of their choosing without deference to the bishop. Neither could they be forced by him to allow him or his agents entry to the abbey, or to attend episcopal <a href="/wiki/Synod" title="Synod">synods</a>. In two follow up letters, Adrian gave the Abbot of St Albans authority to replace the clerks in churches under his jurisdiction with his preferred candidates.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBolton2003a93_250-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBolton2003a93-250">&#91;206&#93;</a></sup> Brooke describes Adrian as "rain[ing] privilege after privilege upon the abbey.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrooke20035_15-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrooke20035-15">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Laudabiliter"><i>Laudabiliter</i></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Adrian_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Laudabiliter">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Adrian_IV_rota.png" class="image"><img alt="colour image of a contemporary document" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/18/Adrian_IV_rota.png/170px-Adrian_IV_rota.png" decoding="async" width="170" height="168" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/18/Adrian_IV_rota.png/255px-Adrian_IV_rota.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/18/Adrian_IV_rota.png/340px-Adrian_IV_rota.png 2x" data-file-width="483" data-file-height="477" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Adrian_IV_rota.png" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The <i><a href="/wiki/Rota_(papal_signature)" title="Rota (papal signature)">rota</a></i> of Pope Adrian IV.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECharter2020_253-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECharter2020-253">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup> The two top quarters contain <i>Petrus</i> and <i>Paulus</i> respectively (for the saints), while the bottom two show the Pope's name (in this case as ADRI–ANUS) and his regnal number.</div></div></div>
<p>Probably Adrian's "most striking"<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2004355_33-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2004355-33">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> donation to England, however, was the <a href="/wiki/Papal_Bull" class="mw-redirect" title="Papal Bull">Papal Bull</a> <i><a href="/wiki/Laudabiliter" title="Laudabiliter">Laudabiliter</a></i> of 1155.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2004355_33-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2004355-33">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> This was supposedly made either while Adrian was in Benevento or had moved on to <a href="/wiki/Ferentino" title="Ferentino">Florento</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPoole1969267_254-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPoole1969267-254">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup> John of Salisbury later claimed credit, writing how "at my request [Adrian] conceded and gave Ireland as a hereditary possession to the illustrious king of the English, Henry II".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2004355_33-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2004355-33">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> This granted the <a href="/wiki/Island_of_Ireland" class="mw-redirect" title="Island of Ireland">island of Ireland</a> to Henry II in <a href="/wiki/Fee_simple" title="Fee simple">fee simple</a>. Adrian's justification was that, since the <a href="/wiki/Donation_of_Constantine" title="Donation of Constantine">Donation of Constantine</a>, countries within Christendom were the Pope's to distribute as he would.<sup id="cite_ref-256" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-256">&#91;note 46&#93;</a></sup> The claim of <i><a href="/wiki/Vicar_of_Christ" title="Vicar of Christ">Vicarius Christi</a></i> which Adrian inherited allowed him, he believed, to wield the temporal power of his subjects through them.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESheehy196148_257-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESheehy196148-257">&#91;211&#93;</a></sup> Sayers suggests that "while some kind of Irish mission" was clearly intended by Adrian, the precise nature of the grant remains unclear.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-259" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-259">&#91;note 47&#93;</a></sup> Duggan, also notes that neither Henry or Adrian ever seems to have referred to it in their own letters again: "whatever Adrian granted, and he did grant something, there is no reliable evidence" as to its nature or what it comprised.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a139_260-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a139-260">&#91;213&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>King Henry claimed to be motivated by a wish to civilise the supposedly unruly Irish.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> The Victorian historian <a href="/wiki/Kate_Norgate" title="Kate Norgate">Kate Norgate</a>, however, has noted that the spiritual community in 12th-century Ireland "flourished", and that the Pope must have known this, as it was only a few years earlier that the Irish church had been reorganised into Archdioceses,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENorgate189337–39_261-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorgate189337–39-261">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup> thus making it a national church in its own right.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERichter1974197_262-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERichter1974197-262">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup> Norgate argues that Adrian's grant was made, not because the church in Ireland needed protecting, but because the Irish lacked a single king and for Christian society to have no single head was an anathema.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENorgate189339–40_263-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorgate189339–40-263">&#91;216&#93;</a></sup> She also notes that it has misleadingly been called a Bull, when it is, in fact, sufficiently informal in its style to be "nothing more than a commendatory letter".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENorgate189340_264-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorgate189340-264">&#91;217&#93;</a></sup> Simple in its approach, the Pope exhorts Henry—if he is to invade Ireland—to do so in the name of the church.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESheehy196163_265-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESheehy196163-265">&#91;218&#93;</a></sup> Other scholars have argued that, whether or not it was an out and out forgery by <a href="/wiki/Gerald_of_Wales" title="Gerald of Wales">Gerald of Wales</a>, Adrian was more likely to have been lukewarm at the idea of invasion at best, as he was equally unenthusiastic at the notion of a Franco-English crusade to the East at the same time.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKostick2013_266-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKostick2013-266">&#91;219&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Bull "granted and gave Ireland to King Henry II to hold by hereditary right, as his letters witness unto this day", and was accompanied by a gold Papal ring "as a token of investiture".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENorgate189320_258-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorgate189320-258">&#91;212&#93;</a></sup> In the early 14th century it was claimed ("by the ordinary [i.e. English] people of Ireland") that Pope had been persuaded<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFrame1989153_267-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFrame1989153-267">&#91;220&#93;</a></sup>—"improperly"<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBroun200737_268-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBroun200737-268">&#91;221&#93;</a></sup>—to grant <i>Laudabiliter</i>, not on the persuasion of Henry II, but on that from the Irish themselves.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFrame1989153_267-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFrame1989153-267">&#91;220&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-269" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-269">&#91;note 48&#93;</a></sup> If he did issue the bull, Adrian may have been influenced by the fact that the Irish church did not pay Peter's Pence, which was a major source of the Papacy's income. He would almost certainly, too, have been aware of <a href="/wiki/Bernard_of_Clairvaux" title="Bernard of Clairvaux">Bernard of Clairvaux</a>'s letter of 1149, in which he wrote that<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHegarty201160_270-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHegarty201160-270">&#91;222&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Never before had he known the like, in whatever depth of barbarism; never had he found men so shameless in regard of morals, so dead in regard to rites, so stubborn in regard of discipline, so unclean in regard of life. They were Christians in name, in fact pagans.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHegarty201160_270-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHegarty201160-270">&#91;222&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Notes Summerson, "the consequences of the bull were still invisible when Adrian died".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESummerson2004_6-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESummerson2004-6">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> As early as 1317, Adrian's grant to Henry was linked in Ireland to his nationality,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERichter1974195_271-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERichter1974195-271">&#91;223&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-275" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-275">&#91;note 49&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/List_of_rulers_of_Tyrone" title="List of rulers of Tyrone">Domnall, King of Tír Eoghain</a> complained that Adrian should be known as "Anti-Christ rather than true Pope".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a138–138_276-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a138–138-276">&#91;227&#93;</a></sup> The Irish called him "a man not only of English descent but also of English inclinations", who "backed his compatriots in what they regarded as the first major confrontation between the two nations",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERichter1974195_271-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERichter1974195-271">&#91;223&#93;</a></sup> whereas the Bishop of Thessaloniki praised Adrian as a <a href="/wiki/Pastor" title="Pastor">pastor</a> ("which is how", comments the scholar <a href="/wiki/Averil_Cameron" title="Averil Cameron">Averil Cameron</a>, "the Byzantines liked to see the Popes").<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECameron201683_159-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECameron201683-159">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Acts_as_pope">Acts as pope</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Adrian_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Acts as pope">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:K.._MOM-Bilddateien._~StiASG2jpgweb._~StiASG_1155~11559999_TTT2(II)-Nr3_r.jpg" class="image"><img alt="colour scan of one of Adrian&#39;s charters" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/K.._MOM-Bilddateien._~StiASG2jpgweb._~StiASG_1155~11559999_TTT2%28II%29-Nr3_r.jpg/170px-K.._MOM-Bilddateien._~StiASG2jpgweb._~StiASG_1155~11559999_TTT2%28II%29-Nr3_r.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="242" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/K.._MOM-Bilddateien._~StiASG2jpgweb._~StiASG_1155~11559999_TTT2%28II%29-Nr3_r.jpg/255px-K.._MOM-Bilddateien._~StiASG2jpgweb._~StiASG_1155~11559999_TTT2%28II%29-Nr3_r.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/K.._MOM-Bilddateien._~StiASG2jpgweb._~StiASG_1155~11559999_TTT2%28II%29-Nr3_r.jpg/340px-K.._MOM-Bilddateien._~StiASG2jpgweb._~StiASG_1155~11559999_TTT2%28II%29-Nr3_r.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3000" data-file-height="4270" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:K.._MOM-Bilddateien._~StiASG2jpgweb._~StiASG_1155~11559999_TTT2(II)-Nr3_r.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Charter of Pope Adrian IV, also beginning <i>Adrianus eps servus servorum dei</i>, dated the Lateran, 30 March 1156. Note the Papal <a href="/wiki/Monogram" title="Monogram">monogram</a>, <i><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bene_Valete&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Bene Valete (page does not exist)">Bene Valete</a></i>, bottom-right-hand corner.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECharter2020_253-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECharter2020-253">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup></div></div></div>
<p>In 1155 the <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_Genoa" title="Republic of Genoa">city state of Genoa</a> approached Adrian and sought him to help them defend their trading rights in the East.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBalard200741_277-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBalard200741-277">&#91;228&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-278" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-278">&#91;note 50&#93;</a></sup> The same year Adrian issued the <a href="/wiki/Decretal" title="Decretal">decretal</a> <i><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dignum_est&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Dignum est (page does not exist)">Dignum est</a></i> which allowed serfs to marry without having to obtain their lord's permission as had traditionally been the case. Adrian's reasoning was that a <a href="/wiki/Sacrament" title="Sacrament">sacrament</a> outweighed a feudal due and that no Christian had the right to stand in the way of another's receiving of a sacrament. This was to become the definitive statement of marriage as a sacrament,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReynolds201640,_451_279-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReynolds201640,_451-279">&#91;229&#93;</a></sup> and remained such until the recodification of <a href="/wiki/1917_Code_of_Canon_Law" title="1917 Code of Canon Law">Canon Law in 1917</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b190_280-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b190-280">&#91;230&#93;</a></sup> The same year, Adrian consecrated the <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Gradisca" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Gradisca">Bishop of Grado</a>, <a href="/wiki/Enrico_Dandolo_(patriarch)" title="Enrico Dandolo (patriarch)">Enrico Dandolo</a> as <a href="/wiki/Dalmatia" title="Dalmatia">Primate of Dalmatia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMadden200736_281-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMadden200736-281">&#91;231&#93;</a></sup> Two years later, Adrian granted him primacy over all the Venetian churches in the Eastern Empire.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMadden201863_282-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMadden201863-282">&#91;232&#93;</a></sup> This has been described as "a remarkable move": The historian <a href="/wiki/Thomas_F._Madden" title="Thomas F. Madden">Thomas F. Madden</a> notes that not only was this the first occasion on which one metropolitan had been given jurisdiction over another, but in doing so Adrian had created the equivalent of an Eastern <a href="/wiki/Patriarch" title="Patriarch">Patriarch</a> in the west.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMadden201280_283-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMadden201280-283">&#91;233&#93;</a></sup> He also confirmed the degradation of <a href="/wiki/Baume_Abbey" title="Baume Abbey">Baume Abbey</a> imposed by Eugenius for its failure to obey a Papal legate.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2004325_n.48_284-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2004325_n.48-284">&#91;234&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Adrian confirmed the prerogatives of the <a href="/wiki/Knights_Templar" title="Knights Templar">Knights Templar</a> and documented in the <i><a href="/wiki/Liber_Censuum" title="Liber Censuum">Liber Censuum</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBellomo2008268_285-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBellomo2008268-285">&#91;235&#93;</a></sup> He also enforced the rules against unfree ecclesiastical elections and condemned ecclesiastics who used physical force against the church.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b191_286-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b191-286">&#91;236&#93;</a></sup> Perhaps reflecting his earlier career, he also promulgated several bulls in favour of the <a href="/wiki/Austin_canon" class="mw-redirect" title="Austin canon">Austin canons</a>. Again, he particularly focussed on houses of his personal association; St Ruf, for example, received at least 10 bulls of privilege. In one of these, he expressed a "special bond of affection" for his old abbey, which he said had been like a mother to him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEgger200327_37-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEgger200327-37">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Adrian argued that, in the troubled succession to <a href="/wiki/Alfonso_the_Battler" title="Alfonso the Battler">Alfonso I of Aragon</a>, even though Alfonso had legally nominated an heir—his brother—because he had not had a son, his brother was not a direct heir to the Kingdom.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMartindale198927_287-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartindale198927-287">&#91;237&#93;</a></sup> This was the context for the projected crusade into Spain as suggested by the Kings of England and France, which Adrian rejected. He did, however, welcome their new friendship.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith200339_288-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith200339-288">&#91;238&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>It was probably Adrian who <a href="/wiki/Canonised" class="mw-redirect" title="Canonised">canonised</a> <a href="/wiki/Sigfrid_of_Sweden" title="Sigfrid of Sweden">Sigfrid of Sweden</a> around 1158, thus making Sigfrid Sweden's <a href="/wiki/Apostle" title="Apostle">apostle</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBeck1940384_289-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBeck1940384-289">&#91;239&#93;</a></sup> Robinson notes that Adrian's fascination with Scandinavia continued into his pontificate, particularly in his efforts to create a Swedish metropolis. He was also keen to defend its church against lay encroachment.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2004349_48-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2004349-48">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> In January 1157 Archbishop Eskil<sup id="cite_ref-290" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-290">&#91;note 51&#93;</a></sup> personally presented a petition to Adrian in Rome, requesting protection from <a href="/wiki/Sweyn_III_of_Denmark" title="Sweyn III of Denmark">King Swein of Denmark</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-291" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-291">&#91;note 52&#93;</a></sup> Adrian both appointed the <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Lund" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Lund">Bishop of Lund</a> his Legate in the region<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2004350_201-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2004350-201">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup> and recognised him as <a href="/wiki/Primate_(bishop)" title="Primate (bishop)">primate</a> over both Sweden and Denmark.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016203_196-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016203-196">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-292" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-292">&#91;note 53&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Other cardinalate appointments of Adrian's included that of Alberto di Morra in 1156. Di Mora, also a canon regular like Adrian, later reigned briefly as <a href="/wiki/Pope_Gregory_VIII" title="Pope Gregory VIII">Pope Gregory VIII</a> in 1187. Boso, already papal chamberlain since 1154, was appointed the same year. Adrian also elevated one Walter to the Pope's own Cardinal Bishopric of Albano; Walter is thought to have been an Englishman—possible also from St Ruf—but very little record of his career has survived. In contrast, his appointment of Raymond des Arénes in 1158 was of a well-known lawyer with an established career under Adrian's predecessors.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b182–183_293-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b182–183-293">&#91;240&#93;</a></sup> These were all worthy additions to the Curial office, argues Duggan, being all men of "experience, academic learning and administrative and diplomatic skill", which in turn reflects the wisdom of the appointer.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b183_294-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b183-294">&#91;241&#93;</a></sup> He may have received the hermit and later saint <a href="/wiki/Silvester_of_Troina" title="Silvester of Troina">Silvester of Troina</a>, whose only recorded journey was from Siciliy to Rome during Adrian's pontificate.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOldfield2014131_295-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOldfield2014131-295">&#91;242&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Adrian continued the reform of the Papacy's finances that had begun under his predecessor in an attempt at boosting revenue,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECollins2009238_296-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECollins2009238-296">&#91;243&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-298" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-298">&#91;note 54&#93;</a></sup> although he regularly had to resort to requesting large loans from major noble families such as the Corsi and <a href="/wiki/Frangipane" title="Frangipane">Frangipane</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWickham2015164,_165_n.170_299-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWickham2015164,_165_n.170-299">&#91;245&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-301" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-301">&#91;note 55&#93;</a></sup> His appointment of Boso as Chamberlain—or camerarius—of the Papal patrimony did much to improve the Papacy's finances by way of streamlining its financial bureaucracy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBlumenthal200423_302-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlumenthal200423-302">&#91;247&#93;</a></sup> However, he also recognised the expense that the Papay was put to defending its own, commenting <i>nemo potest sine stipendiis militare</i>, or "no-one can make war without pay".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMorris1989214_303-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorris1989214-303">&#91;248&#93;</a></sup> Adrian also consolidated the Papacy's position as the feudal lord of the regional baronage;<sup
