id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWickham201554_304-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWickham201554-304">&#91;249&#93;</a></sup> indeed, his success in doing so has been described as "never less than impressive".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBolton2003a178_305-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBolton2003a178-305">&#91;250&#93;</a></sup> In 1157, for example, Adrian made <a href="/wiki/Oddone_Frangipane" title="Oddone Frangipane">Oddone Frangipane</a> donate his castle to him, which Adrian then granted back to Oddone in fee.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWickham201554_304-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWickham201554-304">&#91;249&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-306" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-306">&#91;note 56&#93;</a></sup> occasionally Adrian simply purchased castles and lordships for the papacy, as he did <a href="/wiki/Corchiano" title="Corchiano">Corchiano</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPartner1972194_307-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPartner1972194-307">&#91;251&#93;</a></sup> Adrian received the personal oaths of fealty of a number of north-Roman nobles, thus making them vassals of St Peter.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWhalen2014125_113-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWhalen2014125-113">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> In 1158, for example, for fighting in the <i><a href="/wiki/Reconquista" title="Reconquista">Reconquista</a></i>—"subduing the barbarous peoples and the savage nations, that is, the fury of the Saracens"—Ramon Berenguer, Count of Barcelona was accepted "under St Peter's and our protection".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2004348_308-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2004348-308">&#91;252&#93;</a></sup> In 1159 Adrian ratified an agreement with the civic leadership of <a href="/wiki/Ostia_(Rome)" title="Ostia (Rome)">Ostia</a>—an otherwise semi-independent town—agreed to pay the Pope an annual feudal rent for his lordship.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWickham2015106_309-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWickham2015106-309">&#91;253&#93;</a></sup> Adrian's vassals, and their family and vassals, took oaths of fealty to the Pope, and in doing so the vassal absolved his own vassals of their oaths to him. All now became direct vassals of the Papacy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBolton2003b178–179_310-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBolton2003b178–179-310">&#91;254&#93;</a></sup> One of Adrian's greatest achievements, believed Boso, was acquiring <a href="/wiki/Orvieto" title="Orvieto">Orvieto</a> as a Papal fief, because this city had "for a very long time withdrawn itself from the jurisdiction of St Peter"<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBoltonDuggan2003230–231_311-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoltonDuggan2003230–231-311">&#91;255&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPartner1972194_307-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPartner1972194-307">&#91;251&#93;</a></sup> Adrian, in 1156, was the first Pope to enter Orvieto, emphasised Boso, and to "have any temporal power there".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPartner1972194_307-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPartner1972194-307">&#91;251&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Adrian appears to have been an advocate of the <a href="/wiki/Crusade" class="mw-redirect" title="Crusade">crusade</a> since his abbacy of St Ruf,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith200332_312-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith200332-312">&#91;256&#93;</a></sup> and was equally keen to rekindle the crusading spirit among Christian rulers as Pope. The <a href="/wiki/Second_Crusade" title="Second Crusade">most recent crusade</a> had ended, poorly, in 1150, but Adrian made what has been called a "novel approach" to launching a new one. In 1157 he announced that, whereas previously <a href="/wiki/Indulgence" title="Indulgence">indulgences</a> were available to those who fought in the East, from now on they would be also available to those who supported the war effort without necessarily campaigning abroad. This opened the benefits of crusading up to those who supplied money, men or <a href="/wiki/Materiel" title="Materiel">materiel</a>. However, his proposal, novel or otherwise, appears to have met with little interest, and no further crusading was to take place until 1189.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016472–473_313-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016472–473-313">&#91;257&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETyerman199814–15_314-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETyerman199814–15-314">&#91;258&#93;</a></sup> He did not, however, approve of Crusading within Christendom itself, as when the French and English kings both proposed a crusade into <a href="/wiki/Muslim_Spain" class="mw-redirect" title="Muslim Spain">Muslim Spain</a>, he urged caution upon them. In his January 1159<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith200339_288-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith200339-288">&#91;238&#93;</a></sup> letter <i>Satis laudabiliter</i>, while flattering both kings diplomatically, he advised that "it would seem to be neither wise nor safe to enter a foreign land without first seeking the advice of the princes and people of the area".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b191_286-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b191-286">&#91;236&#93;</a></sup> Indeed, Adrian reminded Henry and Louis of the consequences of badly planned and mismanaged crusades by reference to the Second Crusade—of which Louis had been a leader—reminding him that, there too, Louis had invaded "without consulting the people of the area".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b192_315-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b192-315">&#91;259&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-316" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-316">&#91;note 57&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Adrian also undertook a building program throughout Rome and the patrimony, although Duggan notes that the shortness of his pontificate reduced the amount of his work that remains visible in the 21st century. The work ranged from the restoration of public buildings and spaces to the city's physical defence.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b201_39-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b201-39">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> Boso reported how, for example, "in the church of St Peter [Adrian] richly restored the roof of St. Processo which he found collapsed", while in the Lateran, he "caused to be made a very necessary and extremely large cistern".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBoltonDuggan2003230–231_311-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoltonDuggan2003230–231-311">&#91;255&#93;</a></sup> Due to the peripatetic nature of his pontificate he also built a large number of summer palaces across the patrimony, including at <a href="/wiki/Segni" title="Segni">Segni</a>, Ferentino, <a href="/wiki/Alatri" title="Alatri">Alatri</a>, <a href="/wiki/Anagni" title="Anagni">Anagni</a> and <a href="/wiki/Rieti" title="Rieti">Rieti</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBolton2003b166_317-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBolton2003b166-317">&#91;260&#93;</a></sup> Much of this fortification and building work—particularly in the vicinity of Rome—was for the protection of pilgrims, the safety of whom Adrian was both spiritually and physically reliable for.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPartner1972193–194_318-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPartner1972193–194-318">&#91;261&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Although his pontificate was a relatively short one—four years, six months and 28 days—he spent nearly half that time outside of Rome, either in the enclave of Benevento or journeying around the Papal States and patrimony. Particularly in the early years of the reign, his travels reflected the political context, consisting of "short bursts" as he sought to either meet or avoid the Emperor or William of Sicily as the situation required.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBolton2003b164_319-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBolton2003b164-319">&#91;262&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Personal_philosophy_and_religious_views">Personal philosophy and religious views</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Adrian_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Personal philosophy and religious views">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Ego_adrianus_Catholicae.png" class="image"><img alt="image of Pope Adrian&#39;s signature" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0b/Ego_adrianus_Catholicae.png/220px-Ego_adrianus_Catholicae.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="56" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0b/Ego_adrianus_Catholicae.png/330px-Ego_adrianus_Catholicae.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0b/Ego_adrianus_Catholicae.png/440px-Ego_adrianus_Catholicae.png 2x" data-file-width="764" data-file-height="195" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Ego_adrianus_Catholicae.png" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The <a href="/wiki/Autograph" title="Autograph">autograph</a> of Pope Adrian IV, reading <i>Ego Adrianus Catholicae</i><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECharter2020_253-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECharter2020-253">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup></div></div></div>
<p>The Pope was conscious, comments Sayers, "of the crushing responsibilities" of his office,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> telling John of Salisbury that he felt his Papal tiara to be "splendid because it burned with fire".<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> He was also highly conscious of the historicity of the Petrine tradition; as much as any of his predecessors, says Duggan, Adrian upheld the "unifying and an co-ordinating role of the Papal office", and regularly expressed himself on how he viewed his position as that akin to being a steward:<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b196_320-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b196-320">&#91;263&#93;</a></sup> He also recognised his own smallness within that tradition, telling John of Salisbury that "the Lord has long since placed me between the hammer and the anvil, and now he must support the burden he has placed upon me, for I cannot carry it".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b197_321-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b197-321">&#91;264&#93;</a></sup> This explains his use of the <a href="/wiki/Pope#Title" title="Pope">epithet</a> <i><a href="/wiki/Servus_servorum_Dei" class="mw-redirect" title="Servus servorum Dei">Servus servorum Dei</a></i>, comments Duggan: more than purely rhetorical, it amalgamated his concept of "stewardship, duty and usefulness" in three words.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b197_321-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b197-321">&#91;264&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Having been placed by the Lord's disposition in a lofty watchtower, if the rights of all churches are not preserved whole and unimpaired, we would seem to occupy the place of St Peter Prince of the Apostles unprofitably and to exercise the office of stewardship entrusted to us negligently.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b196_320-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b196-320">&#91;263&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Adrian was keen to emphasise the superiority of the Western Church over that of the East, and lost no opportunity to tell members of that body so.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> 
Adrian described his approach to relations with his political rivals in a letter to the <a href="/wiki/Metropolis_of_Thessaloniki#List_of_bishops" title="Metropolis of Thessaloniki">Archbishop of Thessaloniki</a>. St Peter's authority was indivisible and could not be shared with temporal rulers, he argued. As such—as <a href="/wiki/Petrine_doctrine" class="mw-redirect" title="Petrine doctrine">the descendant of St Peter</a>—neither should he.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> Central to Adrian's view of his Papacy, says Sayers, was the conviction that his court was the highest court in Christendom and, so, the final court of appeal, and he encouraged appeals from many countries.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> In an early letter, defending the principle of Papal Monarchy, he compares <a href="/wiki/Christendom" title="Christendom">Christendom</a> to the human body: all the parts can only function as they should if they have an overarching guide and facilitator. To Adrian, Christian Europe was the body, and the Pope the head.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955236_322-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955236-322">&#91;265&#93;</a></sup> The historian Neil Hegarty has suggested that, on the assumption that <i>Laudabiliter</i> as is extant can be believed, Adrian firmly believed in "enlarging the borders of the Church, setting bounds to the progress of wickedness, reforming evil manners, planting virtue, and increasing the Christian religion".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHegarty201160_270-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHegarty201160-270">&#91;222&#93;</a></sup> Adrian was keen to know what people thought of the Roman Church, and often asked the question of <a href="/wiki/John_of_Salisbury" title="John of Salisbury">John of Salisbury</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> John also recorded Adrian's view's on the Papacy accepting gifts from Christians, which some viewed as <a href="/wiki/Simony" title="Simony">simonaical</a> and evidence of corruption. Adrian, reported John, replied with reference to the <a href="/wiki/The_Belly_and_the_Members" title="The Belly and the Members">fable of the belly</a>. The European analyst Andreas Musolff explains the Pope's position as "deducing from it the church's right to receive, and allocate, the nourishment to the Christian body according to merit and utility".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMusolff200459_323-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMusolff200459-323">&#91;266&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>
Adrian, argues Ullmann, was a man of action with little "inclination towards lengthy theoretical discussions",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955234_80-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955234-80">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> although Norwich argues that he could still be hesitant. For instance, following his radical change of Papal policy at Benevento, he may still not have grasped the significance of what he had done, and certainly not to the extent of radically exploiting the new policy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENorwich1970186_324-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorwich1970186-324">&#91;267&#93;</a></sup> Partner suggests that Adrian was "an able administrator who used able agents".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPartner1972192_325-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPartner1972192-325">&#91;268&#93;</a></sup> He was also a traditionalist;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955234_80-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955234-80">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> a firm follower of Pope Gregory VII, Adrian believed it his duty to not just believe in those ideals, but to enforce them. He also believed in the necessity of reform,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERichter1974206_326-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERichter1974206-326">&#91;269&#93;</a></sup> as his clarification of the marriage sacrament and enforcement of free episcopal elections demonstrates.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b191_286-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b191-286">&#91;236&#93;</a></sup> He was also, like Eugenius had been before him, a firm believer in the supremacy of the Bishops of Rome over both the Empire and other churches, writing how the Papacy,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2004323,_325_327-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2004323,_325-327">&#91;270&#93;</a></sup><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/></p><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Like a diligent mother provides for the individual churches with constant vigilance: all must have recourse to her, as to their head and origin, to be defended by her authority, to be nourished by her breasts and freed of their oppressions.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2004325_328-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2004325-328">&#91;271&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Writings">Writings</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Adrian_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Writings">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The 16th century <a href="/wiki/Jesuit" class="mw-redirect" title="Jesuit">jesuit</a> commentator <a href="/wiki/Augustino_Oldoini" title="Augustino Oldoini">Augustino Oldoini</a>, in his re-edition of <a href="/wiki/Alphonsus_Ciacconius" title="Alphonsus Ciacconius">Alphonsus Ciacconius</a>' <i>Papal History</i>, that Adrian had authored a number of works prior to his election. These included a <a href="/wiki/Treatise" title="Treatise">treatise</a> <i>De Conceptione Beatissimae Virginis</i>, a monograph, <i>De Legationae sua</i> and a <a href="/wiki/Catechism" title="Catechism">catechism</a> for the Scandinavian church.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Some of his correspondence survives. One such letter, from Hildegarde, exhorts him to crush the Roman commune. Joseph Baird and Radd Ehrman, editors of a collection of Hildegard's letters, note that it was "perhaps unneeded", as Adrian placed the city under Interdict almost immediately.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBairdEhrman199420,_44_329-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBairdEhrman199420,_44-329">&#91;272&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-330" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-330">&#91;note 58&#93;</a></sup> Much of Adrian's correspondence with both Archbishop Theobald and John of Salisbury has also been published in collections of the latter's letters.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMillorButler1986ix–xii_331-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMillorButler1986ix–xii-331">&#91;273&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-333" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-333">&#91;note 59&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Adrian's episcopal registry is now lost,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016142_334-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016142-334">&#91;275&#93;</a></sup> although some decretals—formal rulings—survive.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMorris1989402_335-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorris1989402-335">&#91;276&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHeather2014401_336-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeather2014401-336">&#91;277&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-337" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-337">&#91;note 60&#93;</a></sup> These covered such questions as to whether it was possible to restore a priest to his office when he had been responsible for the death of an apprentice, the payment of <a href="/wiki/Tithes" class="mw-redirect" title="Tithes">tithes</a>, and the marriage of the unfree. Adrian's thoughts on tithe payment also made their way into the body of Canon Law,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b185_338-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b185-338">&#91;278&#93;</a></sup> and were, according to Duggan, "recognised by contemporaries as having special significance, and so included in the collections of canon law being assembled at the time".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b185_338-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b185-338">&#91;278&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Personality">Personality</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Adrian_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Personality">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1062260506">.mw-parser-output .quotebox{background-color:#F9F9F9;border:1px solid #aaa;box-sizing:border-box;padding:10px;font-size:88%;max-width:100%}.mw-parser-output .quotebox.floatleft{margin:.5em 1.4em .8em 0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox.floatright{margin:.5em 0 .8em 1.4em}.mw-parser-output .quotebox.centered{overflow:hidden;position:relative;margin:.5em auto .8em auto}.mw-parser-output .quotebox.floatleft span,.mw-parser-output .quotebox.floatright span{font-style:inherit}.mw-parser-output .quotebox>blockquote{margin:0;padding:0;border-left:0;font-family:inherit;font-size:inherit}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-title{background-color:#F9F9F9;text-align:center;font-size:110%;font-weight:bold}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-quote>:first-child{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-quote:last-child>:last-child{margin-bottom:0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-quote.quoted:before{font-family:"Times New Roman",serif;font-weight:bold;font-size:large;color:gray;content:" “ ";vertical-align:-45%;line-height:0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-quote.quoted:after{font-family:"Times New Roman",serif;font-weight:bold;font-size:large;color:gray;content:" ” ";line-height:0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox .left-aligned{text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .quotebox .right-aligned{text-align:right}.mw-parser-output .quotebox .center-aligned{text-align:center}.mw-parser-output .quotebox .quote-title,.mw-parser-output .quotebox .quotebox-quote{display:block}.mw-parser-output .quotebox cite{display:block;font-style:normal}@media screen and (max-width:640px){.mw-parser-output .quotebox{width:100%!important;margin:0 0 .8em!important;float:none!important}}</style><div class="quotebox pullquote floatright" style="width:30em; ; background-color: #FFFFF0;">
<blockquote class="quotebox-quote left-aligned" style="">
<p>For he was very kind, mild, and patient; accomplished in English and Latin, fluent in speech, polished in eloquence. An outstanding singer, and an excellent preacher; slow to anger and swift to forgive; a cheerful giver, lavish in alms, distinguished in every aspect of his character.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b216_339-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b216-339">&#91;279&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
</blockquote>
<p><cite class="left-aligned" style="">Cardinal Boso, <i>Vita Adriani IV</i> (late 1170s)</cite>
</p>
</div>
<p>The historian Colin Morris notes that Adrian's character appears contradictory: "Some historians have seen him as tough and inflexible, but others as a relatively mild man" who could be manipulated by those around him. <sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMorris1989190_25-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorris1989190-25">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> Duggan disputes that he was either a cypher to be manipulated by the cardinals or a <i><a href="/wiki/Prima_donna" title="Prima donna">prima donna</a></i>. Rather, she suggests, he was "a man of discipline, who fitted in with the norms and routines already in place...a man of affairs who had no fixed program, but who responded judiciously to the problems brought before his court. <sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b202_340-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b202-340">&#91;280&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>
Adrian's <a href="/wiki/Chamberlain_(office)" title="Chamberlain (office)">chamberlain</a>, Boso—who later wrote Adrian's <i>Vita</i><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEden2006292_1-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEden2006292-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>—described the Pope as "mild and kindly in bearing, of high character and learning, famous as a preacher, and renowned for his fine voice".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> Julius Norwich describes Adrian as being eloquent, able and with "outstanding good looks".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENorwich1970172_29-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorwich1970172-29">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> The German <a href="/wiki/Antiquarian" title="Antiquarian">antiquarian</a> <a href="/wiki/Ferdinand_Gregorovius" title="Ferdinand Gregorovius">Ferdinand Gregorovius</a> believed that by nature Adrian was "as firm and as unyielding as the granite of his tomb", while Norwich tempers this suggestion, believing that, at least after Benevento, he must have been far more open to the possibilities of change.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENorwich1970186_324-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorwich1970186-324">&#91;267&#93;</a></sup> Duggan wonders whether he deliberately utilised these traits to forward his career. Boso's characterisation, she suggests, "could imply that he was ready to ingratiate himself with the powerful, to make friends and influence people by accommodation and charm".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b198_341-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b198-341">&#91;281&#93;</a></sup> Sayers also suggests that something similar is detectable in the accounts from John of Salisbury,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> a close friend to the Pope since the days of Adrian's curial visits.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEgger200320_23-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEgger200320-23">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-342" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-342">&#91;note 61&#93;</a></sup><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1062260506"/></p><div class="quotebox pullquote floatleft" style="width:30em; ; background-color: #FFFFF0;">
<blockquote class="quotebox-quote left-aligned" style="">
<p>For I call on the Lord Adrian to witness than no one is more miserable than the Roman Pontiff, nor is any condition more wretched than his. . . . He maintains that the papal throne is studded with thorns, that his mantle bristles with needles so sharp that it oppresses and weighs down the broadest shoulders . . . and that had he not feared to go against the will of God he would never have left his native England.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENorwich1970177_105-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorwich1970177-105">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
</blockquote>
<p><cite class="left-aligned" style="">John of Salisbury, <i><a href="/wiki/Polkratkus" class="mw-redirect" title="Polkratkus">Polkratkus</a></i>, Book VIII, xxiii.</cite>
</p>
</div>
<p>Adrian's own view of his office, suggests Sayers, is summed up in his own words: his "<a href="/wiki/Pallium" title="Pallium">pallium</a> was full of thorns and the burnished mitre seared his head", would have supposedly preferred the simple life of a canon at St Ruf.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> However, he also respected those who worked beneath him in the curia's officialdom; on one occasion he instructed that "we ought to reward such persons with ecclesiastical benefices when we conveniently can".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMorris1989549_343-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorris1989549-343">&#91;282&#93;</a></sup> This approach is reflected in the elevation of fellow Englishmen—Walter, and potentially John of Salisbury—to high office. Brooke suggests that, ultimately, Adrian "had not forgot his origins; he liked to have Englishmen about him".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrooke200312_235-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrooke200312-235">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>His increasing control over Rome and the Patrimony demonstrate that he was an effective organiser and administrator, argues the scholar Edward Whalen.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWhalen2014125_113-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWhalen2014125-113">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> Duggan argues that Adrian's strength of personality can be seen in his very election: in spite of being an outsider, a newcomer and lacking the support or patronage of an Italian noble house he attained the apotheosis of his church. And, she says, these were the qualities that made him independent.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a153_59-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a153-59">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>His biographer, Cardinal Boso,<sup id="cite_ref-345" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-345">&#91;note 62&#93;</a></sup> was a close friend who visited Adrian at Rome between November 1155 and June the following year.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESheehy196158_346-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESheehy196158-346">&#91;284&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-348" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-348">&#91;note 63&#93;</a></sup> John's feelings for Adrian were strong enough, indeed, for them to have been compared with, for example, that of <a href="/wiki/Richard_the_Lionheart" class="mw-redirect" title="Richard the Lionheart">Richard the Lionheart</a> for <a href="/wiki/Philip_II_of_France" title="Philip II of France">Philip of France</a>. Boswell notes that in John's <i><a href="/w/index.php?title=Metalogicon&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Metalogicon (page does not exist)">Metalogicon</a></i>, he used terms reminiscent of those used by chroniclers to describe relations between the Kings.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBoswell198048,_216_n.30_349-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoswell198048,_216_n.30-349">&#91;286&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-351" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-351">&#91;note 64&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Modern historiography has not always been complimentary to Adrian. Freed argues that Adrian was capable of both shameful and specious arguments in his dispute with Barbarossa.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016252_352-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016252-352">&#91;288&#93;</a></sup> Likewise, <a href="/wiki/David_Abulafia" title="David Abulafia">David Abulafia</a> has called Adrian "petulant",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAbulafia198856_167-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbulafia198856-167">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup> and Latowsky has criticised his "sarcastic" manner towards Barbarossa.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELatowsky2013163_353-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELatowsky2013163-353">&#91;289&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Death">Death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Adrian_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Tomb_of_Pope_Adrian_IV.jpg" class="image"><img alt="Black and white photo of Adrian&#39;s tomb" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3e/Tomb_of_Pope_Adrian_IV.jpg/170px-Tomb_of_Pope_Adrian_IV.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="119" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3e/Tomb_of_Pope_Adrian_IV.jpg/255px-Tomb_of_Pope_Adrian_IV.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3e/Tomb_of_Pope_Adrian_IV.jpg/340px-Tomb_of_Pope_Adrian_IV.jpg 2x" data-file-width="592" data-file-height="416" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Tomb_of_Pope_Adrian_IV.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Tomb of Pope Adrian IV</div></div></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1062260506"/><div class="quotebox pullquote floatright" style="width:25em; ; background-color: #FFFFF0;">
<blockquote class="quotebox-quote left-aligned" style="">
<p>At Anagni Hadrian proclaimed the emperor excommunicate and a few days later, to cool himself down [during the hot weather] he started off for a certain fountain along with his attendants. When he got there he drank deeply and at once (according to the story), a fly entered his mouth, stuck to his throat, and could not be shifted by any device of the doctors: and as a result, the pope died.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMaxwell-Stuart199797_14-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaxwell-Stuart199797-14">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
</blockquote>
<p><cite class="left-aligned" style=""><a href="/wiki/Burchard_of_Ursperg" title="Burchard of Ursperg">Burchard of Ursperg</a>'s <i><a href="/w/index.php?title=Chronicon_Urspergensis&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Chronicon Urspergensis (page does not exist)">Chronicon Urspergensis</a></i>, <a href="https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/circa" class="extiw" title="wiktionary:circa">c.</a> 1159</cite>
</p>
</div>
<p>By autumn 1159 it may have been clear to Adrian's household and companions that he had not long to live. This may have been at least in part caused by the stresses of his pontificate, suggests Norwich, which although short, was difficult.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENorwich1970186_324-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorwich1970186-324">&#91;267&#93;</a></sup> Pope Adrian died in Anagni<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016258_354-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016258-354">&#91;290&#93;</a></sup>—to where he had retired for security against the Emperor<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcBrien1997_226-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBrien1997-226">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup>—from <a href="/wiki/Peritonsillar_abscess" title="Peritonsillar abscess">quinsy</a><sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup><sup id="cite_ref-356" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-356">&#91;note 65&#93;</a></sup> on 1 September 1159. He died, says Norwich, "as many Popes had died before him, an embittered exile; and when death came to him, he welcomed it as a friend".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENorwich1970186_324-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorwich1970186-324">&#91;267&#93;</a></sup> He was buried three days later<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> in an "undistinguished third-century sarcophagus"<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENorwich1970186_324-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorwich1970186-324">&#91;267&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Porphyry_(geology)" title="Porphyry (geology)">porphyry</a> tomb of his own choosing.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2004322_85-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2004322-85">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-357" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-357">&#91;note 66&#93;</a></sup> In 1607, the Italian archaeologist <a href="/wiki/Giovanni_Francesco_Grimaldi" title="Giovanni Francesco Grimaldi">Giovanni Francesco Grimaldi</a> excavated the crypt and in the process opened Adrian's tomb. He described the body, still well preserved, as that of an "undersized man, wearing Turkish slippers on his feet and, on his hand, a ring with a large emerald", and dressed in a dark <a href="/wiki/Chasuble" title="Chasuble">Chasuble</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENorwich1970186_324-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorwich1970186-324">&#91;267&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcBrien1997_226-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBrien1997-226">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At the time of Adrian's death, argues Partner, "imperial pressure on the papacy was stronger than it had been since the time of Henry V, and it is not surprising that the cardinals were unable to agree about his successor".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPartner1972203_358-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPartner1972203-358">&#91;292&#93;</a></sup> It is likely that in the months presaging his death the cardinals were aware of the likelihood of a schism occurring soon afterwards;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson199679_175-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson199679-175">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup> Freed suggests that thanks to Adrian's own policies, "a split in the College of Cardinals was thus almost preordained", regardless of the Emperor's input.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016257_359-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016257-359">&#91;293&#93;</a></sup> Ullmann suggests that it was the ideological positions of individual cardinals which was shaping—and introducing faction to—the Curia in the last months of Adrian's pontificate.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann2003124_192-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann2003124-192">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup> However, Norwich states that Frederick Barbarossa orchestrated the schism himself.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENorwich1995132_360-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorwich1995132-360">&#91;294&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In September 1159—now leading the Emperor's opponents<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>—Adrian had agreed ("but did not swear") to excommunicate Barbarossa.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016257_359-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016257-359">&#91;293&#93;</a></sup> He also did not have time to judge the request of Scottish Legates who had been in Rome since that summer, who were requesting the <a href="/wiki/Diocese_of_St_Andrews" class="mw-redirect" title="Diocese of St Andrews">Diocese of St Andrews</a> be made a <a href="/wiki/Metropolitan_Archdiocese" class="mw-redirect" title="Metropolitan Archdiocese">metropolitan</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarrow1994223_361-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrow1994223-361">&#91;295&#93;</a></sup> and the <a href="/wiki/Beatification" title="Beatification">beatification</a> of <a href="/wiki/Waltheof_of_Melrose" title="Waltheof of Melrose">Waltheof of Melrose</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBirkett201055_362-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBirkett201055-362">&#91;296&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-364" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-364">&#91;note 67&#93;</a></sup> One of his final acts was the blessing of his preferred successor, Bernard, Cardinal-Bishop of Porto,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-365" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-365">&#91;note 68&#93;</a></sup> testified <a href="/wiki/Eberhard_II_von_Otelingen" title="Eberhard II von Otelingen">Eberhard, Bishop of Bamberg</a> to the Conclave.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson199680_194-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson199680-194">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup> This, suggests Sayers, could have been Adrian's "masterstroke". The election of Bernard—as a candidate acceptable to the Emperor—may have avoided the future schism.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> That the Cardinals ended up agreeing with Adrian's choice indicates he had chosen wisely, argues Baumgartner.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaumgartner2003b32_115-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaumgartner2003b32-115">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-366" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-366">&#91;note 69&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Pope Adrian was buried in St Peter's on 4 September 1159. Present were three Imperial ambassadors who had been in attendance on the Pope when he died. They were Otto of Wittelsbach—who had tried to beat up Cardinal Roland at Besançon—<a href="/w/index.php?title=Guido_of_Biandrate&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Guido of Biandrate (page does not exist)">Guido of Biandrate</a> and <a href="/w/index.php?title=Heribert_of_Aachen&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Heribert of Aachen (page does not exist)">Heribert of Aachen</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016257_359-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016257-359">&#91;293&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-368" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-368">&#91;note 70&#93;</a></sup> However, as soon as the Emperor heard of the Pope's death, says Madden, he "sent a group of agents and a great deal of money to Rome" in an attempt to secure the election of a successor with pro-Imperial sympathies.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMadden201295_369-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMadden201295-369">&#91;299&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Later_events">Later events</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Adrian_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Later events">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Pope_Alexander_III.png" class="image"><img alt="late-medieval colour depiction of Alexander iii" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c4/Pope_Alexander_III.png/170px-Pope_Alexander_III.png" decoding="async" width="170" height="225" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c4/Pope_Alexander_III.png/255px-Pope_Alexander_III.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/c/c4/Pope_Alexander_III.png 2x" data-file-width="332" data-file-height="440" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Pope_Alexander_III.png" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>15th-century <a href="/wiki/Illustrated_manuscript" class="mw-redirect" title="Illustrated manuscript">depiction</a> of Pope Alexander III, taken from the <i><a href="/wiki/Nuremberg_Chronicle" title="Nuremberg Chronicle">Nuremberg Chronicle</a></i> of 1493<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENuremberg_Chronicle2020_370-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENuremberg_Chronicle2020-370">&#91;300&#93;</a></sup></div></div></div>
<p>The meeting between Hadrian and the city envoys of June 1159 may have discussed the next Papal election, as Adrian was known to have been accompanied by 13 cardinals who supported his pro-Sicilian policy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson199679–80_371-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson199679–80-371">&#91;301&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-373" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-373">&#91;note 71&#93;</a></sup> Cardinal Roland's election to succeed Adrian saw both the conflict with the Empire intensify and the alliance with William of Sicily solidify.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarber1992236_158-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarber1992236-158">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup> The schism had a knock-on effect with regard to Papal policy in Italy, making it little more than a passive observer to events on its own doorstep.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMatthew1992263_374-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMatthew1992263-374">&#91;303&#93;</a></sup> Papal scholar Frederic Baumgartner argues that a disputed election was the inevitable consequence whenever pope and emperor had a falling out.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaumgartner2003b32_115-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaumgartner2003b32-115">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> Relations between Barbarossa and Manuel, already poor—Manuel saw his western counterpart as an "embarrassment", suggests Magdalino, after his falling out with Adrian—ended completely following the death of Manuel's German wife, <a href="/wiki/Bertha_of_Sulzbach" title="Bertha of Sulzbach">Bertha of Sulzbach</a>, earlier in 1159.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMagdalino2004622_375-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMagdalino2004622-375">&#91;304&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Following Adrian's death, comments Barber, "the consequence for the church was another long and bitter schism".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarber1992103_229-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarber1992103-229">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup> Tensions between different parties led to a double election, with "mutually unacceptable candidates".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson199678–79_376-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson199678–79-376">&#91;305&#93;</a></sup> This led to what <a href="/wiki/Frank_Barlow_(historian)" title="Frank Barlow (historian)">Frank Barlow</a> has called "disgraceful scenes" taking place in Rome, but, with neither side powerful enough to overcome the other, each appealed to the European powers.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarlow1936264_377-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarlow1936264-377">&#91;306&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>
Although the Papal forces were insufficient to defeat Barbarossa outright, the war in Lombardy gradually turned against the Emperor, and following the recognition of the Kings of France and England, the military situation became more evenly balanced.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWhalen2014127_225-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWhalen2014127-225">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup> However, peace was not established between the Papacy, the Empire, Sicily and the Byzantine Emperor until Barbarossa was defeated at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Legnano" title="Battle of Legnano">Battle of Legnano</a> in 1176 and the following year's <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Venice" title="Treaty of Venice">Treaty of Venice</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMetcalfe2009173_378-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMetcalfe2009173-378">&#91;307&#93;</a></sup> The schism continued until the election of Pope Alexander III in 1180. During this time the Emperor's chancery distributed a series of fake letters—some of which purported to have been written by Adrian—in defence of the Imperial candidate.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016215_379-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016215-379">&#91;308&#93;</a></sup> One such letter, supposedly to Archbishop <a href="/wiki/Hillin_of_Falmagne" title="Hillin of Falmagne">Hillin of Trier</a>, comments Latowsky, "is of particular interest since it contains a deliberately erroneous rewriting of Charlemagne's assumption of the imperial title".In it, Adrian launches into a diatribe, condemning the German kings who owe everything to the Papacy yet refuse to understand that. This letter, argues Latowsky, was clearly intended to enrage its Imperial audience.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELatowsky2013161,_164_380-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELatowsky2013161,_164-380">&#91;309&#93;</a></sup> Another letter, from the Emperor to Archbishop, called Adrian's church as "a sea of snakes", a "den of thieves and a house of demons" and Adrian himself as "he who claims to be the Vicar of Peter, but is not".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELatowsky2013163_353-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELatowsky2013163-353">&#91;289&#93;</a></sup> Adrian, in turn, says the Emperor is "out of his mind".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELatowsky2013170_381-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELatowsky2013170-381">&#91;310&#93;</a></sup> Containing as they did summaries of each sides' arguments at Besançon, they are most interesting, argues Freed, for indicating what Barbarossa to have believed to have been the most important of his and Adrian's arguments.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016215_379-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016215-379">&#91;308&#93;</a></sup><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/></p><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>From the time when friendship was established at Benevento between the lord Pope Hadrian and William of Sicily. contrary to the honour of God's Church and of the empire, great division and discord have arisen (not without cause) among the cardinals...blinded by money and many promises and firmly bound to the Sicilian, wickedly defended the treaty.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson199679_175-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson199679-175">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Further afield, war was threatening between England and France.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGodman2000173_382-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGodman2000173-382">&#91;311&#93;</a></sup> Lands lost by Adrian to Sicily at the Treaty of Benevento were eventually regained by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Innocent_III" title="Pope Innocent III">Pope Innocent III</a> early in the next century, by which time the Kingdom of Sicily had merged with the Empire.<sup id="cite_ref-384" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-384">&#91;note 72&#93;</a></sup> Innocent saw Adrian's original grant as detracting from the privilege of the <a href="/wiki/Apostolic_See" class="mw-redirect" title="Apostolic See">Apostolic See</a>, and made concerted and eventually successful efforts to evict the Empire from southern Italy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPowell200419–20_385-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPowell200419–20-385">&#91;313&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="1159_Papal_Conclave">1159 Papal Conclave</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Adrian_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: 1159 Papal Conclave">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/1159_papal_election" title="1159 papal election">1159 papal election</a> was disputed, and the College of Cardinals split along sectarian lines, between the "Sicilian"—so-called as its members wished to continue the pro-William policy of Adrian—and "Imperial" sympathisers. The former supported the candidacy of Cardinal Roland; the latter that of Ottaviano de Monticelli. Roland was elected Pope Alexander III. The result was not accepted by his opponents, who elected an <a href="/wiki/Antipope" title="Antipope">Antipope</a>, <a href="/wiki/Antipope_Victor_IV_(1159%E2%80%931164)" title="Antipope Victor IV (1159–1164)">Victor IV</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson199653_386-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson199653-386">&#91;314&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-388" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-388">&#91;note 73&#93;</a></sup> whom John of Salisbury had previously mocked.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWhalen2014127_225-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWhalen2014127-225">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup> The Imperial party disagreed with the new policy of <i>rapprochement</i> with Sicily and favoured the traditional alliance with the Empire.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson199679_175-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson199679-175">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup> A missive of the Imperial party of electors claimed that Adrian was a "dupe" of the Sicilian faction within the cardinals.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson1996470_184-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson1996470-184">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup> Indicative of Barbarossa's attitude towards his candidate was his willingness to serve Victor as he had objected to serving Adrian, for example by holding the Antipope's horse and kissing his feet.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016257_359-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016257-359">&#91;293&#93;</a></sup>The conclave to elect Adrian's successor, says Ullmann, was a "riotous and undignified spectacle".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann2003124_192-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann2003124-192">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup> Alexander was elected by two-thirds of the college, while Victor's support declined from nine to five cardinals.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson199653_386-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson199653-386">&#91;314&#93;</a></sup> Two further antipopes were elected before Alexander's death in 1181 and a unity candidate was found.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarber1992103_229-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarber1992103-229">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup> Alexander was left a problematic legacy by Adrian, who had guaranteed a powerful enemy for the Papacy in the Emperor. Alexander managed to negotiate successive crises, however, and held its own.{<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Within a year, Emperor Manuel had recognised Alexander,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMagdalino199364_389-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMagdalino199364-389">&#91;316&#93;</a></sup> as had the English King Henry, although the latter waited nine months to do so.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2007169_390-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2007169-390">&#91;317&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-392" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-392">&#91;note 74&#93;</a></sup>  Although Octavian received less curial support in the conclave, he had the support of the Roman commune. As a result, Alexander and his supporters were forced into the sanctuary of the <a href="/wiki/Leonine_City" title="Leonine City">Leonine</a> <a href="/wiki/Borgo_(rione_of_Rome)" title="Borgo (rione of Rome)">Borgho</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann2003124–125_393-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann2003124–125-393">&#91;319&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy_and_assessment">Legacy and assessment</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Adrian_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: Legacy and assessment">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Pope_Hadrian_IV.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4c/Pope_Hadrian_IV.jpg/220px-Pope_Hadrian_IV.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="222" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4c/Pope_Hadrian_IV.jpg/330px-Pope_Hadrian_IV.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/4/4c/Pope_Hadrian_IV.jpg 2x" data-file-width="405" data-file-height="409" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Pope_Hadrian_IV.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>In the 14th century Adrian was recorded in St Albans' Book of Benefactors, which, suggests Bolton, "ensured that the memory of the English Pope would remain forever"</div></div></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1062260506"/><div class="quotebox pullquote floatright" style="width:30em; ; background-color: #FFFFF0;">
<blockquote class="quotebox-quote left-aligned" style="">
<p>The archives of his pontificate are not extensive, but the picture emerges of an assiduous administrator, a man of strange vision and singular purpose, though of balanced judgement, who became something of a role model for later popes.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
</blockquote>
<p><cite class="left-aligned" style="">Jane E. Sayers</cite>
</p>
</div>
<p>In the 14th century Adrian was recorded in St Albans' <i>Book of Benefactors</i>, which, suggests Bolton, "ensured that the memory of the English Pope would remain forever".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBolton2003a96_394-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBolton2003a96-394">&#91;320&#93;</a></sup> The 19th-century antiquarian <a href="/wiki/Bishop_Stubbs" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop Stubbs">Bishop Stubbs</a> described Adrian IV as, in his view, "a great pope; that is a great constructive pope, not a controversial one, like those who preceded and followed".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStubbs1887151_395-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStubbs1887151-395">&#91;321&#93;</a></sup> Walter Ullmann argues that Adrian's pontificate "left an imprint upon contemporary Europe which is not yet fully recognised".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955233_66-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955233-66">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> One reviewer has described Adrian's pontificate as, traditionally, "largely remembered as the answer to a trivia question (who was the only English pope?) or as a footnote in Anglo-Irish history".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHakkenberg2004212_396-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHakkenberg2004212-396">&#91;322&#93;</a></sup> On the other hand, the <a href="/wiki/Dixie_Professor_of_Ecclesiastical_History" title="Dixie Professor of Ecclesiastical History">Dixie Professor of Ecclesiastical History</a>, <a href="/wiki/Christopher_N._L._Brooke" title="Christopher N. L. Brooke">Christopher N. L. Brooke</a> argues that, on account of the number of different countries he dwelt in over the years, he illustrates the cosmopolitan nature of 12th-century religion.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrooke20033_397-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrooke20033-397">&#91;323&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrooke20036_16-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrooke20036-16">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The timing of Adrian's pontificate, suggests Ullmann, was significant because it made him the first pope on which what Ullmann calls "newly released forces"—the recently crowned King Henry and Emperor Frederick.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955234_80-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955234-80">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-398" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-398">&#91;note 75&#93;</a></sup> On the other hand, argues Egger, Adrian—by rejecting the request of Kings Louis and Henry to crusade in Spain—undoubtedly prevented the secular power from embarrassing themselves: "one can only speculate about what might have happened, but it is not improbable that Adrian spared the Iberian peninsula a disaster on the scale of the second crusade to the East".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith200339_288-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith200339-288">&#91;238&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Morris argues that while "in a short pontificate, Hadrian did more than any of his predecessors to secure the papal position in central Italy...he was much less successful in his conduct of relations with the empire".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMorris1989191_123-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorris1989191-123">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> Norwich too strikes a cautionary note. While agreeing that Adrian was "the greatest pope since Urban II", he argues that it would be difficult not to "tower...above the string of mediocrities who occupied the throne of St Peter during the first half of the century, just as he himself is overshadowed by his magnificent successor".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENorwich1970186_324-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorwich1970186-324">&#91;267&#93;</a></sup> Duggan argues that, although "the future of the papacy was to be determined by other men and other events, but he had played his part in guiding it securely through an extremely critical phase of its long history".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a155_99-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a155-99">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Ullmann has called Adrian "diplomatically very well versed and experienced, dispassionate and purposeful in his government".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann2003122_117-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann2003122-117">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> Adrian—"the pope of action", says Ullmann—was Papal theory "become eminently practical".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955233_66-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955233-66">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> He was not, however, a dictator. Likewise, suggests the historian <a href="/wiki/Christopher_Tyerman" title="Christopher Tyerman">Christopher Tyerman</a>, Adrian's new approach to drumming up support for a crusade in 1157 became "a pivotal feature of crusading from the reign of Innocent III onwards".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETyerman199815_399-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETyerman199815-399">&#91;324&#93;</a></sup> Innocent himself recognised the debt which he owed to Adrian's pontificate, argues Ullmann.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955234_80-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955234-80">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> Innocent codified Adrian's changes to the Imperial coronation as official procedure.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955241_137-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955241-137">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup> Even the Besançon affair, suggests Ullmann, casts him in a positive light, and "by his dignified stand against Staufen attacks appears like a rock in comparison with the clamorous Germans".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955244_187-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955244-187">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The period immediately preceding Adrian's pontificate, argues <a href="/wiki/Malcolm_Barber" title="Malcolm Barber">Malcolm Barber</a>, was one where "even without a direct imperial threat, Roman feuds, Norman ambitions and incompetently led crusades could reduce grandiose papal plans to ashes".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarber199299_67-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarber199299-67">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> The Papacy itself was one of permanent struggle and conflict, although scholars disagree as to the degree of culpability the papacy had for this. Duggan suggests that "no fair judgment should be reached without recognising the vulnerability" of the papacy itself. His policy, if he can be said to have one, she says, was shaped by events rather than shaping them.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a109_65-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a109-65">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> Ullmann argues that there was "a perfect concordance between Adrian's symbolic actions and his acts of government".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955239_400-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955239-400">&#91;325&#93;</a></sup> Adrian and his pro-Scicillian cardinals, suggests Duggan, became scapegoats in 1159 for the subsequent conflict.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a107_180-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a107-180">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Sayers describes Adrian IV as "a true son of the reforming papacy".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> However, the <a href="/wiki/Gregorian_Reform" title="Gregorian Reform">Papal reform movement</a> does not appear to have had faith that Adrian would carry out its program, as leading reformers of the day—such as Gerhoh of Reichersberg and <a href="/wiki/Hildegard_of_Bingen" title="Hildegard of Bingen">Hildegard of Bingen</a>, for example—sought the church's renewal other ways.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDaniel20048_401-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDaniel20048-401">&#91;326&#93;</a></sup> Chris Wickham credits Adrian with beginning the process by which the popes expanded their Patrimony. Adrian brought Rome brought back under firm Papal control<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWickham201539,_51_402-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWickham201539,_51-402">&#91;327&#93;</a></sup>—with considerable success, argues Wickham,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWickham2015419_403-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWickham2015419-403">&#91;328&#93;</a></sup> and also expanded the Papal estate around the city, particularly in the northern <a href="/wiki/Lazio" title="Lazio">Lazio</a> region.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWickham201539,_51_402-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWickham201539,_51-402">&#91;327&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Although his Papacy was shorter than either Eugenius III or Alexander III he bought more castles and lordships within papal jurisdiction than either of them, and in a more onerous political context.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBolton2003b178_404-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBolton2003b178-404">&#91;329&#93;</a></sup> He was also a tougher pope than his two immediate predecessors, says Wickham<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWickham2015445_405-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWickham2015445-405">&#91;330&#93;</a></sup> and his was an "extremely formative" Papacy, says Sayers, and his policy of reform was a legacy taken up again by reforming popes of the 13th century.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> His Papacy though, suggests Eden, was "fraught with political intrigue and conflict".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEden2006293_95-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEden2006293-95">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> Adrian has been described as having "theocratic pretensions",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPixton2001727_406-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPixton2001727-406">&#91;331&#93;</a></sup> although it was also during his pontificate that the term "Vicar of Christ" became a common synonym for the Pope.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcBrien1997_226-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBrien1997-226">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Henry Summerson suggests that on his death, Adrian "left a high reputation", and quotes the <i><a href="/wiki/Dictionary_of_National_Biography" title="Dictionary of National Biography">Dictionary of National Biography</a></i> in saying that he also became "something of a role model for later popes".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESummerson2004_6-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESummerson2004-6">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> The scholar Michael Frassetto suggests that blame for the poor relations between Adrian and Frederick can be placed as much upon their advisors—Roland and Reinald specifically—who both "stressed principle over compromise".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFrassetto2001296_407-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFrassetto2001296-407">&#91;332&#93;</a></sup> Summerson also notes that although England provided no subsequent popes, relations between that country and the Papacy remained strong after Adrian's death and into the 13th century.<sup id="cite_ref-408" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-408">&#91;note 76&#93;</a></sup> Adrian's generous treatment of St Albans also had repercussions. He had granted it privileges of such breadth and grandeur—which were confirmed by his successors—that they caused rancour and jealousy in the English church.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBolton2003a95_409-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBolton2003a95-409">&#91;333&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Ullman suggests that it was Adrian who began the restoration of the <a href="/wiki/Papal_monarchy" class="mw-redirect" title="Papal monarchy">Papal monarchy</a> that would reach its apotheosis under Innocent III,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann2003122_117-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann2003122-117">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> while Bolton argues that "only Innocent, the great Roman, realised the value to the papacy of following where Adrian, the unique Englishman, had led".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBolton2003b178_404-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBolton2003b178-404">&#91;329&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Nicholas_Breakspear_School" title="Nicholas Breakspear School">Nicholas Breakspear School</a> in St Albans, England, built in 1963, is named in his honour.<sup id="cite_ref-410" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-410">&#91;334&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="See_also">See also</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Adrian_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: See also">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/List_of_popes" title="List of popes">List of popes</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Cardinals_created_by_Adrian_IV" title="Cardinals created by Adrian IV">Cardinals created by Adrian IV</a></li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Notes">Notes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Adrian_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=28" title="Edit section: Notes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist">
<div class="mw-references-wrap mw-references-columns"><ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-3"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-3">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Although the historian R. L. Poole speculates that Adrian may have been born many years later, as he was sent on a lengthy trip to Scandinavia in 1152, and, says Poole, "so onerous a task would hardly have been imposed on a man past middle age"<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPoole1969295_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPoole1969295-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-9"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-9">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The only reliable source closest to his own life is that by Cardinal Boso in the <i><a href="/wiki/Liber_Pontificalis" title="Liber Pontificalis">Liber Pontificalis</a></i>, but, comments Brooke, "this is exceedingly terse on his early life", noting only his nationality and voyage to France for learning.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrooke20034_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrooke20034-8">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-17"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-17">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Christopher Brooke also surmises that, with a surname such as "Camera", he was likely to have been a clerk.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrooke20036_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrooke20036-16">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-20"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-20">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Richard may have been a married priest, as, during his son's later struggle with the Holy Roman Emperor, the latter asserted it was the case as a slur.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> This was not unusual in the 12th-century church. The historian <a href="/w/index.php?title=Anne_Llewellyn_Barstow&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Anne Llewellyn Barstow (page does not exist)">Anne Llewellyn Barstow</a> says: "Despite six hundred years of decrees, canons, and increasingly harsh penalties, the Latin clergy still...lived with their wives and raised families. In practice, ordination was not an impediment to marriage; therefore some priests did marry even after ordination."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarstow198245_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarstow198245-18">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> The practice was generally confined to lower orders, such as <a href="/wiki/Subdeacon" title="Subdeacon">subdeacons</a>, however, it may be that de Camera was one such.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrooke200319_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrooke200319-19">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-26"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-26">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Also called St Rufus,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEParry199718_4-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEParry199718-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> this was an important regional <a href="/wiki/Motherhouse" title="Motherhouse">motherhouse</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZinn19951481_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZinn19951481-24">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMorris1989190_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorris1989190-25">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-30"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-30">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Eugenius may also have been an <a href="/wiki/Anglophile" title="Anglophile">Anglophile</a>, as it appears that he once told John of Salisbury "that he found the English admirably fitted to perform any task they turned their hand to, and thus to be preferred to all other races – except, he added, when frivolity got the better of them".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENorwich1970172_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorwich1970172-29">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-34"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-34">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The first had been the theologian <a href="/wiki/Robert_Pullen" title="Robert Pullen">Robert Pullen</a>, to the Cardinalate of <a href="/wiki/SS._Martino_e_Silvestro" class="mw-redirect" title="SS. Martino e Silvestro">SS. Martino e Silvestro</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2004355_33-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2004355-33">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-38"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-38">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Poole suggests that in the early-to-mid-portion of the 12th century, Merton was much favoured by <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Diocese_of_S%C3%A9ez#Bishops" title="Roman Catholic Diocese of Séez">John, Bishop of Séez</a>, and it had been Bishop John who had appointed Robert Pullen to the <a href="/wiki/Archdeacon_of_Rochester#High_Medieval" title="Archdeacon of Rochester">Archdeaconry of Rochester</a>; by 1149, Pullen was Papal Chancellor and, Poole suggests, listened favourably to the recommendation of Séez regarding Breakspeare, particularly as he was a fellow countryman.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPoole1969295_2-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPoole1969295-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-41"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-41">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The scholar Damian Smith notes a pre-existing connection between Breakspear and the region through his predecessor at St Ruf, Olegarius, abbot between 1113 and 1118,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcCrank1978162,_nn.15+17_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcCrank1978162,_nn.15+17-40">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> and who had then been unwillingly promoted to the <a href="/wiki/Bishopric_of_Barcelona" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishopric of Barcelona">Bishopric of Barcelona</a> and subsequently the <a href="/wiki/Archbishopric_of_Tarragona" class="mw-redirect" title="Archbishopric of Tarragona">Archbishopric of Tarragona</a>. He also became an important advisor to Ramon Berenguer IV, Count of Barcelona.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith200333_36-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith200333-36">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-46"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-46">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">St Ruf's observances, suggests Egger, became popular with the Catalonian nobility as well as the church, so was "of no small advantage to the order".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEgger200325_45-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEgger200325-45">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-63"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-63">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Breakspear was one of two English clerics who influenced Scandinavian Christianity at this time; the other was <a href="/wiki/Henry_(bishop_of_Finland)" title="Henry (bishop of Finland)">Henry, Bishop of Uppsala</a>, who was originally from St Albans.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESingleton199818_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESingleton199818-62">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-71"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-71">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">However, the contemporary chronicler of 12th-century Popes Boso, noted that—notwithstanding the hostility of Rome to the Pope at this time—these elections were peaceful and unanimous, although Boso did describe the election of Eugenius III as taking place in "unexpected harmony".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson199678_69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson199678-69">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> Sicily, a Papal fief in name only, provided its theoretical Papal lords with neither status nor income.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELoud2004465_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELoud2004465-70">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> Adrian's predecessor, Anastasia, had possessed a good grasp of the intricacies of Roman politics and had used his knowledge to maintain peaceful relations between all factions where possible.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMaxwell-Stuart199797_14-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaxwell-Stuart199797-14">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
