<li id="cite_note-75"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-75">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The Roman commune's idiosyncrasies included creating its own <a href="/wiki/Carolingian_minuscule" title="Carolingian minuscule">minuscule script</a>, a unique <a href="/wiki/Calendar_era" title="Calendar era">dating system</a> and its own <a href="/wiki/Seal_(emblem)" title="Seal (emblem)">seal</a> and <a href="/wiki/Chancery_(medieval_office)" title="Chancery (medieval office)">chancery</a>. Wickham calls the Roman project both radical and unparalleled.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWickham2015446_73-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWickham2015446-73">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-81"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-81">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">A student of <a href="/wiki/Abelard" class="mw-redirect" title="Abelard">Abelard</a>'s at the <a href="/wiki/Cathedral_school" title="Cathedral school">cathedral school</a> of <a href="/wiki/Notre-Dame_de_Paris" title="Notre-Dame de Paris">Notre-Dame de Paris</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENorwich1970173_78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorwich1970173-78">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> Arnold has been described by the <a href="/wiki/Church_history" title="Church history">ecclesiastic historian</a>, <a href="/wiki/Philip_Schaff" title="Philip Schaff">Philip Schaff</a>, an "unsuccessful ecclesiastical and political agitator, who protested against the secularization of the Church"<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESchaff1994119_79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchaff1994119-79">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> with what Ullman terms a "fiery call for a return to apostolic poverty", says Ullmann.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955234_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955234-80">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-90"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-90">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The German historian Walter Norden argues that Manuel was<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Hoping with the help of the papacy to rise to dominion over the west and thereupon over the papacy itself; the popes were dreaming with the support of the Comneni of becoming the masters of the Byzantine church and thereupon of the Byzantine Empire.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENorden1904101_89-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorden1904101-89">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-100"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-100">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">A new pope would usually be elected in the Lateran, but this was currently holding Anastasius' body.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955237_96-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955237-96">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-102"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-102">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">At this point, a simple majority vote sufficed until 1179, when the <a href="/wiki/Third_Lateran_Council" class="mw-redirect" title="Third Lateran Council">Third Lateran Council</a> increased the required majority to two-thirds.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaumgartner2003a167–168_101-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaumgartner2003a167–168-101">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-103"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-103">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Ullmann notes that this was "a somewhat unusual feature at the time, as many popes were not in orders at the time of their election".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955237_96-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955237-96">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-106"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-106">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The <a href="/wiki/Leonine_City" title="Leonine City">Leonine City</a> was built by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Leo_III" title="Pope Leo III">Pope Leo III</a> in the 9th century. It was located to the northwest of Rome, beyond the city walls; as such, it was officially outside the jurisdiction of the commune.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETwyman200349_74-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETwyman200349-74">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-110"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-110">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">During this period, the Norman <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Sicily" title="Kingdom of Sicily">Kingdom of Sicily</a> comprised not just the island of <a href="/wiki/Sicily" title="Sicily">Sicily</a>, but most of Southern Italy—<a href="/wiki/Apulia" title="Apulia">Apulia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Calabria" title="Calabria">Calabria</a> and <a href="/wiki/Longobardia" title="Longobardia">Longobardia</a>—and <a href="/wiki/Malta" title="Malta">Malta</a>. Both the Papacy and the Holy Roman Empire also claimed the region.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrown201511–12_109-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown201511–12-109">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-111"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-111">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">This was not only a spiritual punishment for the city; the drying up of the <a href="/wiki/Pilgrimage" title="Pilgrimage">pilgrimage</a> industry during <a href="/wiki/Lent" title="Lent">Lent</a> led to economic hardship.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-119"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-119">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The sources have not recorded the precise date of the meeting, but the 7th,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESayers2004_5-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESayers2004-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> 8th or 9th of the month all seem most likely.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016140_118-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016140-118">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-127"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-127">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The affair was presumably settled diplomatically, as Barbarossa accepted his crown from Adrian in spite of it, and yet the picture remained in the Lateran until at least the 16th century.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJong2013163_126-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJong2013163-126">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-132"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-132">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The precise nature of these "old documents" remains unclear; it is possible that they were fragments of Constantine's "Donation".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016141_130-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016141-130">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-134"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-134">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Both parties' perspectives were recorded subsequently by sympathetic chroniclers. That of the Empire recorded a meeting characterised by peace and harmony, while Boso describes a tense situation in which the honour of the Papacy was at stake.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson1996464_122-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson1996464-122">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-138"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-138">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Not only was the Emperor anointed on the shoulder, rather than as previously, on the head, but Adrian introduced a lower-quality oil. He also altered the procedural order of the ceremony. Since 1014, Emperors were first consecrated by the cardinals and then anointed by the Pope, within the <a href="/wiki/Coronation_Mass" title="Coronation Mass">Coronation Mass</a> itself. Adrian, however, decided that the anointing should come before the mass. Explains Ullmann, "the underlying reason being that only an ecclesiastical ordo was conferred during mass, but since the future emperor did not receive an ordo, the unction had to be performed before the mass".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955241_137-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955241-137">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-149"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-149">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Adrian was deliberately rude to William, suggests the scholar Donald Matthew, for example referring to him as <i>dominus</i> (lord) of Sicily rather than as King. Boso later tried to conceal Adrian's role errors, claiming that it had been the fault of his cardinals.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMatthew199263,_65_147-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMatthew199263,_65-147">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELoud201238_148-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELoud201238-148">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-151"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-151">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The south of Italy, particularly Apulia, had a large Greek population, with <a href="/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_church" class="mw-redirect" title="Greek Orthodox church">Greek Orthodox churches</a>, who "played important roles in the administration of the Norman kingdom", notes Duggan, "and Byzantine emperors had not yet abandoned hopes of re-establishing some control over the south".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a120_150-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a120-150">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-157"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-157">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The coastal towns of Apulia had large Greek populations.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMagdalino2008639_156-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMagdalino2008639-156">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-168"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-168">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Benevento was a Papal enclave inside Sicilian southern Italy, so Adrian was unable to escape easily.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAbulafia198856_167-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbulafia198856-167">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-171"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-171">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">This did not apply to the mainland of Southern Italy, but, Barber points out, this was effectively the <i>status quo</i> in any case: Popes only entered the region four times between 1154 and 1189.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarber1992236_158-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarber1992236-158">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-173"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-173">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">These were greater powers than the Emperor enjoyed in his realm, and thus embittered relations between him and the Pope further.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMatthew1992193_172-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMatthew1992193-172">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-182"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-182">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Duggan suggests that once he had been elected as emperor, all he needed was a puppet pope, and that Adrian's policy of keeping the papacy on an independent course, was an "intolerable provocation".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a154_181-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a154-181">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-185"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-185">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The two parties used different terms to mean the same thing. Both originally used <i>beneficium</i> to mean a feudal holding. The church had begun using the term <i>feudum</i> a few years earlier; the Empire had not. It is, suggests Robinson, "difficult to believe" that the Curia had forgotten this.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson1996470_184-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson1996470-184">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-189"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-189">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Who may have been Cardinal Roland, suggests Duggan.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a129_188-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a129-188">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-191"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-191">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Besançon was an important Imperial town, being the capital of <a href="/wiki/Upper_Burgundy" title="Upper Burgundy">Upper Burgundy</a>, and the Emperor's wedding celebration was attended by representatives of the crowned heads of Christendom; thus, notes Norwich, his falling out with the Pope was an exceedingly public one.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENorwich1970180_174-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorwich1970180-174">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-193"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-193">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Roland had been a student of <a href="/wiki/Gratian" title="Gratian">Gratian</a> and had gone on to teach at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Bologna" title="University of Bologna">University of Bologna</a>; with Roland, comments Ullmann, "the dynasty of the great lawyer Popes was to begin".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann2003124_192-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann2003124-192">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-199"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-199">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Eskil at the time was <i>persona non grata</i> in the Empire, and Freed suggests that Adrian—while never placing his thoughts on paper—"probably suspected Frederick of complicity in Eskil's capture as well as laxity in procuring his release".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016203–204_197-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016203–204-197">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup> Duggan suggests that it was wholly political, as Adrian's recent elevation of the Lund Archbishopric had "effectively detached the region from the ecclesiastical jurisdiction" of the Empire.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a127_198-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a127-198">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-211"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-211">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Freed notes that <i>beneficium</i> had "three different meanings in the twelfth century: 'good deed', as Adrian pointed out in his conciliatory letter of June 1158; 'an ecclesiastical living', the modern English 'benefice'; and 'fief'".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016204_210-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016204-210">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-215"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-215">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The <a href="/wiki/Latinist" class="mw-redirect" title="Latinist">Latinist</a> Peter Godman has described Rainauld as "a fomenter of schism and despiser of the Church".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGodman2000198_214-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGodman2000198-214">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-218"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-218">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Specifically, as <i>suggestione perversi hominis zizania seminantis</i> or "the machinations of a depraved man sowing <a href="/wiki/Tare_(tufted_grass)" class="mw-redirect" title="Tare (tufted grass)">tares</a>", a biblical term used in relation to Lucifer.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGodman201448_217-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGodman201448-217">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup> John of Salisbury called him schismatic-in-chief during Adrian's lifetime, and as late as 1166 still believed Reinald to be the "greatest among the locusts of the beasts".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGodman2000198_214-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGodman2000198-214">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-222"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-222">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The ecclesiastical historian Z. N. Brooke has argued that the difference in meaning, while subtle to modern ears, would have been plain to medieval observers; he suggests that "the significance of [Adrian's choice of words] might have escaped us, if we had not got the Emperor's violent protest against it".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrooke1989140_n.2_221-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrooke1989140_n.2-221">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-228"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-228">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The basis of the Emperor's claims was, in Duggan's words, the belief that "virtually all civic administration was deemed to be derived from, and thus subject to, imperial authority".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a135_n.147_227-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a135_n.147-227">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-237"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-237">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Bolton notes, though, that even before their own "loyal son" become Pope, abbots of St Alban's "did everything in their power to advance and promote the position of their house".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBolton2003a79_236-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBolton2003a79-236">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-252"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-252">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The title of the grant came, as was traditional with Papal documents, from the opening words. In this case, the first sentence is<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBolton2003a93_250-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBolton2003a93-250">&#91;206&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBoltonDuggan2003313_251-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoltonDuggan2003313-251">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup>
<table>

<tbody><tr>
<td><i>Incomprehensibilis et ineffabilis diuine miseratio maiestatis nos hac providentie ratione in apostolice sedis administratione constituit...</i>
</td>
<td>The incomprehensible and ineffable mercy of the Divine Majesty has established us in the apostolic see for this providential reason...
</td></tr></tbody></table></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-256"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-256">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">This was the same basis for the Papacy's claim to precedence over the Holy Roman Empire, as the inheritor of the ancient <a href="/wiki/Roman_Empire" title="Roman Empire">Roman Empire</a> in the West.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarber1992102_125-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarber1992102-125">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> <i>Laudabiliter</i> was, however, the only time in the 12th century that the Donation was interpreted as allowing interference in foreign countries.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2004355–356_255-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2004355–356-255">&#91;210&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-259"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-259">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">This is compounded by the fact that no copy of <i>Laudabiliter</i> is extant.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENorgate189320_258-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENorgate189320-258">&#91;212&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-269"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-269">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Specifically, says the early medievalist <a href="/w/index.php?title=Robin_Frame&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Robin Frame (page does not exist)">Robin Frame</a>, that of <a href="/wiki/Diarmait_Mac_Murchada" class="mw-redirect" title="Diarmait Mac Murchada">MacMurrough</a>, <a href="/wiki/King_of_Leinster" class="mw-redirect" title="King of Leinster">King of Leinster</a>, who, having been expelled from his lands, asked Henry for aid, who then "informed Pope Adrian of MacMurrough's suggestion and asked for permission to enter that land".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFrame1989153_267-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFrame1989153-267">&#91;220&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-275"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-275">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Nationality has been impossible to avoid in the subsequent historiography, comments the medievalist J. D. Hosler:<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHosler200770_272-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHosler200770-272">&#91;224&#93;</a></sup><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>The controversy over the veracity of Hadrian's bull has been rather intense, due not only to the vagaries surrounding the evidence but also no doubt to the <a href="/wiki/The_Troubles" title="The Troubles">ubiquity of past and present</a> Anglo-Irish hostilities".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHosler200770_272-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHosler200770-272">&#91;224&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote> <a href="/wiki/J._H._Round" class="mw-redirect" title="J. H. Round">J. H. Round</a> called it "one of the hottest historical controversies that this generation has known",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERound1899171_273-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERound1899171-273">&#91;225&#93;</a></sup> while Hosler argues that the bull "would today be a non-issue was it not for Henry II's eventual conquest of Ireland sixteen years later in 1171".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHosler2007238_274-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHosler2007238-274">&#91;226&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-278"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-278">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Whilst at the same time making overtures to the Manuel I to formalise a trading treaty with Genoa.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBalard200741_277-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBalard200741-277">&#91;228&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-290"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-290">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Eskil was a personal friend of <a href="/wiki/Bernard_of_Clairvaux" title="Bernard of Clairvaux">Bernard of Clairvaux</a> and had been responsible for originally introducing <a href="/wiki/Monasticism" title="Monasticism">monasticism</a> to Denmark and Sweden.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2004350_201-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2004350-201">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-291"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-291">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Swein was also a vassal of the Holy Roman Emperor.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2004350_201-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2004350-201">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-292"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-292">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Eventually the three provinces agreed on Uppsala as the centre of the proposed metropolitan, and Alexander III conferred it in 1164.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2004349_48-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2004349-48">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-298"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-298">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">This process involved Boso, as Chamberlain examining old rent books from the archives in the hope of discovering lost Papal dues,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECollins2009238_296-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECollins2009238-296">&#91;243&#93;</a></sup> and Adrian also ordered the keeping of more precise fiscal records,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWhalen2014125_113-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWhalen2014125-113">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> which became the nucleus of the <i><a href="/wiki/Liber_Censuum" title="Liber Censuum">Liber Censuum</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b182_297-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b182-297">&#91;244&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-301"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-301">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">For example, a loan from Pietro Frangipane in 1158 of 1,000 <a href="/wiki/Mark_(currency)" title="Mark (currency)">marks</a>, for which a number of Papal castles had been given as security, was not paid off until 1190.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWickham2015228_300-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWickham2015228-300">&#91;246&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-306"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-306">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Wickhma argues this was Papal policy: "aristocrats in Rome had to resign themselves to having possession of their lands, not full property".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWickham201554_304-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWickham201554-304">&#91;249&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-316"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-316">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">He did however defer rather than deny the request for a crusade, as he intimated that, if his conditions were met—i.e. that western princes were invited to do so—he would then countenance their crusade.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b192_315-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003b192-315">&#91;259&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-330"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-330">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">This particular letter has been described as expressing "ideas which are themselves highly abstruse...where either the imagery is so contorted as to be intelligible only to the recipient, if in fact even to him, or as to be deliberately indecipherable", as it contains references to a large number of <a href="/wiki/Allegorical" class="mw-redirect" title="Allegorical">allegorical</a> animals.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBairdEhrman199420,_44_329-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBairdEhrman199420,_44-329">&#91;272&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-333"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-333">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">In an early letter, Theobald berates Adrian for sending a messenger who "has either betrayed his trust out of malice or lost them [Theobald's letters] through negligence or has falsely pretended that they were lost", and advises Adrian to "speak to your messenger at your good pleasure and, if it please you, give him instructions to carry out your business with greater caution and fidelity".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMillorButler198620_332-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMillorButler198620-332">&#91;274&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-337"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-337">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">This compares, however, to 713 from Alexander III's pontificate.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMorris1989402_335-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorris1989402-335">&#91;276&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="margin-left:0.1em; white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Please_clarify" title="Wikipedia:Please clarify"><span title="The text near this tag may need clarification or removal of jargon. (June 2020)">clarification needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-342"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-342">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Indeed, <a href="/wiki/Reginald_Lane_Poole" title="Reginald Lane Poole">R. L. Poole</a> has asserted that John had an illegitimate son, which he named Adrian in the Pope's honour, and that, had the child been a girl, he was going to name her Adriana.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPoole1969271_243-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPoole1969271-243">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-345"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-345">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Boso also wrote <i>vitae</i> of Innocent II, Eugenius III and Alexander III.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuscombeRiley-Smith2004770_344-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuscombeRiley-Smith2004770-344">&#91;283&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-348"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-348">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Sheehy describes Boso as "one of the most influential clerics in the England of his day", who later became <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Chartres" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Chartres">Bishop of Chartres</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESheehy196158_346-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESheehy196158-346">&#91;284&#93;</a></sup> He was also a good friend to the English <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_Canterbury" title="Archbishop of Canterbury">Archbishop of Canterbury</a>, <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Becket" title="Thomas Becket">Thomas Becket</a>, although there is no evidence, says Duggan, to suggest that he was either English or a nephew of Pope Adrian's, as Victorian historians believed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2004181,_181_n.24_347-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2004181,_181_n.24-347">&#91;285&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-351"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-351">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The gender historian <a href="/wiki/James_Boswell" title="James Boswell">James Boswell</a>, writing in his <i><a href="/wiki/Christianity,_Social_Tolerance,_and_Homosexuality" title="Christianity, Social Tolerance, and Homosexuality">Christianity, Social Tolerance, and Homosexuality</a></i>, emphasises, however, that this does not make them, in modern terminology, "latently homosexual", but rather that <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>John and Hadrian, who conceived of their love for each other in terms very like those used to describe the passion between the two kings, would have reacted somewhat differently to homosexual sentiments than modern churchmen, who would not describe their friendships with men in such terms".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBoswell198048_350-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoswell198048-350">&#91;287&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-356"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-356">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The 16th-century <a href="/wiki/Martyrologist" class="mw-redirect" title="Martyrologist">martyrologist</a> <a href="/wiki/John_Foxe" title="John Foxe">John Foxe</a> later said that this had been caused by the Pope choking on a fly.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOliver1945392_355-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOliver1945392-355">&#91;291&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-357"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-357">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Porphory, a purple stone, was much associated with the <a href="/wiki/Emperor_Hadrian" class="mw-redirect" title="Emperor Hadrian">Emperor Hadrian</a> and had first been chosen by <a href="/wiki/Innocent_II" class="mw-redirect" title="Innocent II">Innocent II</a> and was subsequently adopted by <a href="/wiki/Lucius_III" class="mw-redirect" title="Lucius III">Lucius III</a> and <a href="/wiki/Urban_III" class="mw-redirect" title="Urban III">Urban III</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2004322_85-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2004322-85">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-364"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-364">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">As the scholar Helen Birkett points out, the Pope to whom the original request was sent was not to be the one who approved it. This, Birkett argues, is an example of the Papacy being seen to make grants as an institution rather than as individual popes.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBirkett201055,_55_n.60_363-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBirkett201055,_55_n.60-363">&#91;297&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-365"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-365">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The letter of the law, as expressed by Cardinal <a href="/wiki/Pietro_Senex" title="Pietro Senex">Pietro Senex</a> in 1130 that "there must be no mention of the successor before the Pope is buried", was widely ignored.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson199680_194-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson199680-194">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-366"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-366">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Between leaving Anagni and arrival in Rome the Cardinals agreed to either elect someone from within their number who was acceptable to all, or someone from outside the college if they could not.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016258_354-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016258-354">&#91;290&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-368"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-368">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">They were under instructions from the Emperor to either make peace with Adrian or, failing that, with the Roman commune.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreed2016256_367-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreed2016256-367">&#91;298&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-373"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-373">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Anne Duggan, while acknowledging that there were a small number of cardinals who can be closely identified with either side, suggests that most of Adrian's officials were neither Imperialists or Sicilians.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a108_372-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2003a108-372">&#91;302&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-384"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-384">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The Papal lands would effectively stay this way until the <a href="/wiki/Capture_of_Rome" title="Capture of Rome">annexation of the Papal States</a> in 1870.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECollins2009256_383-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECollins2009256-383">&#91;312&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-388"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-388">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Not to be confused with the Antipapacy of Cardinal Gregorio Conti, who had previously taken the title <a href="/wiki/Antipope_Victor_IV_(1138)" title="Antipope Victor IV (1138)">Victor IV</a> in 1138.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPalgrave1921730_387-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPalgrave1921730-387">&#91;315&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-392"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-392">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Henry subsequently assumed that he had Alexander's backing vis a vis the English church as a <i><a href="/wiki/Quid_pro_quo" title="Quid pro quo">quid pro quo</a></i> for supporting his pontifical election. This was not, however, to be, and Alexander later condemned <a href="/wiki/Constitutions_of_Clarendon" title="Constitutions of Clarendon">many of Henry's policies</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson2004356_391-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson2004356-391">&#91;318&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-398"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-398">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Ullmann labels Henry II and Frederick I "classic examples of the reinvigorated royalist-lay ideology".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955234_80-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUllmann1955234-80">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-408"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-408">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Culminating, suggest Summerson, after <a href="/wiki/John_of_England" class="mw-redirect" title="John of England">King John</a> made the country a Papal fief in 1215.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESummerson2004_6-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESummerson2004-6">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
</ol></div></div>
